correlation coefficient between the estimate and the ground truth stands at 0.921.
To probe why the estimates of the noise function differ between the (GR+LR) and LE runs of the
Lp objective optimisation scheme, we assessed whether the estimated noise from the regularised run, lay
within the eigenspace of the LE run: we found that over 99.9% of ρreg
est(x) could be explained by our chosen
eigenbasis, thus it is a feature of the choice of regularisation that causes the specific shape observed in the
graph of ρreg
est(x) as seen in Panel D and F of figure 7. But as we noted, the total regularisation for both the
order and the noise accounts for only about 3% of the total objective’s magnitude. This indicates that the
Lp objective around the true ρgt(x), is very flat and cannot easily distinguish between different functional
forms; adding the LR regularisation clearly creates a local minimum around the observed optimal ρreg
est(x) in
the (GR+LR) run. However, the fact that when we replace the Laplacian regularisation with an eigenbasis
decomposition, which doesn’t change the objective and merely restricts the parameter space, we get an
estimate which clusters strongly around the true values of ρgt(x) indicates to us that the signal of the true
noise is present in the dataset, but that this signal is so weak that it cannot easily be distinguished from
other nearby functions in parameter space. We could potentially ameliorate these effects by the addition of
a more principled regularisation; for example, we could consider adding a regularisation term of the form
R(Φ) =
1
m
Pm
i=1
τ(xi)2
ρ(xi)2 which is the drift part of the Onsager-Machlup action [66] averaged over the data;
in theory, minimising this action will minimise the relative entropy between the inferred path law from a
driftless reference law.
The potential unidentifiability of our optimisation problem reduces to how the model balances drift and
diffusion, so an objective that depends directly on such a quantity might help amplify the noise signal relative
to the drift; furthermore, minimising the Girsanov action is equivalent to maximising the path entropy, thus,
in theory, the optimal parameters will assume the least structure beyond what is required by the data. In
this paper we will not explore other possibilities for setting up this optimisation problem, but we note that
this is highly relevant and interesting future work. Assuming the KL-divergence is a good choice of measure
for this problem, the Lp objective being flat around ρgt(x) might be empirical evidence that this optimisation
problem is theoretically unidentifiable and the fact that either the (GR+LR) or LE run give results that
align with the ground truth is the result of some other artifact in the data, that need not be present for all
processes or in all samples.
What we have shown with this final example is that both formulations of the optimisation problem given
by the Lp objective are capable of accurately reconstructing the dynamics of the NL process given in (104).
Both formulations reconstruct a smooth order that is highly correlated with the true ordering, indicating that
we can truly resolve the first-order dynamics in the presence of spatially varying noise arising from a complex
non-linear SDE. The noise estimate from the regularised (GR+LR) optimisation is clearly a smooth function,
as desired, and captures the form of the true noise function accurately (maximal initially, and then decays
the further from the centre one goes); although, it overestimates the diffusion (underestimates the diffusion
gradient) in the central portion of the dynamics and fails to resolve the uptick in diffusion beyond ∥x∥2 > 2π.
In contrast, the estimated noise from the LE optimisation is highly clustered around the true noise function
through the entire range of the process and, furthermore, is capable of resolving the uptick in diffusion
beyond ∥x∥2 > 2π. However, the LE noise estimate is highly noisy, likely due both to choosing K = 250,
which is much larger than needed, and to the signal from the noise in the data being very weak, making
it difficult to identify a single smooth function within the eigenspace. Both the Lq and Lp objectives are
first attempts to build an optimisation framework that leverages the rigid structure and interpretable design
of ODKs to learn unknown first- and second-order dynamics from data generated by a Markov stochastic
process and which comes with very little a priori dynamical information.
37


5
Conclusion
In this paper, we have introduced Ordered Diffusion Kernels (ODKs) as a novel class of local kernels for
computing transition probabilities and the infinitesimal generator for an arbitrary Itˆo SDE. We assume that
we have data sampled from a dynamical system not in steady state and with known initial conditions; we
do not require any additional dynamical information, such as known rates of evolution, a mechanistic model
for the underlying process, exact sampling times, or a known time-sampling distribution. As a prototypical
example of such data, we use single-cell RNA sequencing data (scRNA-seq), which captures a gene expression
landscape of a cell population and which we assume is not in steady state, i.e.
a stem cell undergoing
differentiation. Representing or learning dynamics on this data is thus a highly underdetermined problem
and is often approached by building hugely expressive inference machines to overwhelm the task; these often
rely on deep neural networks or complex probabilistic models. The issues with such models are that, due to
the lack of dynamic constraints, one cannot be sure that massively over-parameterised methods have learnt
coherent dynamics that faithfully correlate with the true underlying dynamics. We designed ODKs as a
simple alternative to these methods, the expansion of the normalised integral operator of Theorem 3.1 tells
us exactly what underlying process we are approximating, that being the Itˆo SDE with infinitesimal generator
L = τ
κ∇l · ∇+ D∆, and the restriction to sample-to-sample transitions constrains the model to not learn
unjustifiable out of distribution dynamics. We took great care in making sure ODKs were as interpretable and
modular as possible, allowing practitioners a great deal of freedom to tune the parameters or tailor the ODK
to the specific data they are studying. We emphasised this modularity through the various extensions of ODK
that we discussed: we constructed local-Ordered Diffusion Kernels (lODKs) to represent non-gradient flow
dynamics (section 3.1); multi-Ordered Diffusion Kernels (mODKs) are compatible with multiple ordering
functions (section 3.1.1; anisotropic-Ordered Diffusion Kernels (anODKs) allow for anisotropic diffusions
(section 3.2; and self-tuning-Ordered Diffusion Kernels (stODKs) allow for symmetric bandwidths of the
form ρ(x)ρ(y) (section 3.2.1).
For this latter extension, we also strengthened the asymptotic results of
Variable Bandwidth Kernels [10] from weak to strong convergence. Fundamentally, all these extensions rely
on what we call an ordering function, which we conceptualise as a relaxed form of a potential in which
only the implied directionality is important, i.e. ODKs will bias transitions towards higher-order samples.
Since we assume the initial conditions are known — which for the example of scRNA-seq would be some a
priori knowledge of the gene expression of stem cells — we can always construct an ordering as the geodesic
distance — on an undirected connected neighbourhood graph of the data — of any sample from these initial
conditions; so in the abscence of any dynamical information we can still use ODKs to represent dynamics
that will correlate with the underlying dynamics that generated the data. In section 4.3, we leveraged the
rigid structure of these dynamic representations to build two loss functions, namely the Lq and Lp objectives,
which we can fit into a simple gradient descent optimisation scheme to learn the drift and diffusion parameters
of data sampled on an SDE. We demonstrated on data sampled from an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck and Non-Linear
SDEs that we were capable of learning the true underlying drift and diffusion of the underlying process, even
when the diffusion was state-dependent.
Our results follow on from a considerable body of work that formalises the intuition that (un)weighted
graph Laplacians produce representations that are coherent with the geometry of the underlying manifold.
Most directly relevant to our work is TMDmap by Banisch et al. [2], who leveraged ideas from the α-density
regularised Diffusion Maps kernels [21] to aim the transitions towards any target measure; however, this
creates an inextricable coupling between the first- and second-order terms of the Laplacian. The structure of
ODKs allows us to avoid this coupling, allowing us the freedom to set the drift and diffusion independently of
one another. Also relevant is the work of Berry & Sauer in their paper on Local Kernels [11]; here the most
general formulation of the asymptotic convergence of (localised) kernels is provided, and is a result we use
repeatedly in the proofs given in Appendix A. However, the framing of local kernels was from the perspective
of vector fields, which requires abundant dynamical information to infer with confidence. To summarise, when
working with data generated by a dynamical system and in which little dynamical information is available,
we believe the reformulation of the unknown dynamics in terms of an ordering function is both a substantial
relaxation of the initial inference problem, while still being sufficient to represent the underlying dynamics.
ODKs are then a practical reformulation of Local Kernels within this framework, giving practitioners a tool
to convert a minimal representation of the dynamics into a practical tool for further analysis, or a dynamic
representation from which to formulate an inference problem. The well-understood limiting behaviour of
38


ODKs then gives users confidence in the fidelity of this representation and clarity about how each component
input to the ODK is resolved in the data.
Limitations:
The choice of an optimal parameterisation for kernel methods is historically a very challeng-
ing task since one needs to balance a choice of parameterisation where the kernel is most useful for further
analysis, and one must ensure that the kernel is fitting the specific sample of the data that one has. Our
conceptualisation of ODKs as forming a representation of the underlying dynamics that generated the data
constrains the notion of what good looks like, but for data that is very sparse or inhomogeneously sampled,
it is not clear that the best representation of the dynamics would be possible given a specific sampling of
the data. Thus, for data of this sort, the intrinsic sensitivity of kernel methods to the sampling of the data
cannot be ignored, and must be dealt with carefully. One of the strongest traits of kernel methods is their
non-dependence on the dimensionality of the data, since the only step in the evaluation of a kernel that
depends on the dimension is the initial computation of a pairwise distance matrix, this is the essence behind
calling the evaluation of a kernel a representation of the data; however, what is often left unsaid is how ker-
nels are affected if the distance metric itself is unreliable. Particularly, for data that is embedded in Rn with
n ≫100, the Euclidean metric is known to struggle in distinguishing distances; particularly, the concentra-
tion of measure phenomena [56] shows that as n →∞, the distance to the nearest and furthest samples in a
bounded domain will tend to the same value, i.e. the ability for the Euclidean metric to discriminate between
distances collapses as the dimension increases — this is particularly relevant to scRNA-seq data in which
one is considering the RNA counts associated to many thousands of genes. In such situations, one should
also take care to ensure that the chosen distance metric is informative enough for the kernel to resolve the
data as desired. We also note that anisotropic diffusions do not fit naturally within the framework of ODKs
as they do with local kernels; this can be seen in the expansion of Theorem 3.4 where the drift is coupled to
the anisotropic diffusion tensor, much like the drift is coupled to the diffusion in TMDmaps. We mollified
this coupling by proposing to use the Strang Splitting scheme to compose a drifted ODK with isotropic
diffusion and a symmetric anisotropic local kernel; this has the effect of removing this coupling but adds a
free parameter α ∈(0, 1) that one must choose when deciding how to split the diffusion between the two
operators. The choice of α is not arbitrary since if it is made too small or too large one of the two kernels
will fail to resolve on a the data; furthermore, if your number of samples is large, m ≫104, computing
the product of three dense matrices of this size will take a not insignificant amount of time, and thus may
preclude the use of such a scheme in an iterative framework such as gradient descent in the optimisation
schemes of section 4.3.
Future Directions:
The most obvious future direction is to apply ODKs to the motivating example
of scRNA-seq data.
The abundance of so-called PseudoTime methods in the scRNA-seq literature [89,
40, 62] makes the idea of an ordering function almost canonical within the field and allows us to jump
straight into exploratory analysis of the dynamics. Due to the limited dynamical information available, high
dimensionality, and inhomogeneous data sampling, this will be a challenging test for ODK; its success or
failure will, more generally, indicate where the limits of this and similar kernel methods lie. However, we note
that the method of Diffusion PseudoTime [40] leverages the work of Coifman & Lafon [21] to construct a
diffusion distance on scRNA-seq data constructed through a symmetric bandwidth diffusion kernel [10]; this
method is considered one of the most reliable and state-of-the-art methods from the PseudoTime literature,
thus giving us cause to believe that ODKs can be applied to scRNA-seq data effectively. Of particular interest
is the application of the Lq or Lp objective introduced in section 4.3; this may allow us to simultaneously infer
the potential and the state-dependent diffusion that underlie differentiation. Allowing for state-dependent
diffusion would already be a significant advancement in the field, since diffusion is almost always assumed to
be constant in the literature. In this paper, we do not prove that either the Lq or Lp objective is identifiable
from our assumed observations; providing such a result, or understanding why it is not possible, would give
much-needed context for the sort of scientific statements one can make after running these optimisations
(see related work of Lavenant et al. [55]). Furthermore, we formulated these objectives as simple maximum-
likelihoods; it is possible, and indeed probable, that different and better formulations exist that can leverage
ODKs’ representations of the dynamics to greater effect and potentially improve our chances of identifiability.

A
Proofs of Main Results
To complete the proofs of section 3, we will use two Lemmas that we take from [21], which were initially
formulated in the PhD Thesis of Belkin [3]; however, we state them in the more convenient form that
Coifman & Lafon provide. We define and use two systems of coordinates on our manifold d-dimensional
Riemannian manifold M: the normal coordinates — constructed implicitly using the exponential map
— which we denote by (s1, . . . , sd); and the projection coordinates — constructed extrinsically using the
implicit function theorem to define a neighbourhood where we can project the embedded manifold onto a
d-dimensional affine subspace of Rn — which we denote by (u1, . . . , ud). For a much more rigorous discussion
of the construction of both of these coordinate charts, please consult appendix B.5.
The first Lemma gives an expression for the components of the normal coordinates, in terms of the
components of the projection coordinates:
Lemma A.1 (Conversion of Coordinates [21]). For any x ∈M, for sufficiently small ε and y ∈B(x, ε1/2)
(the closed ball centred as x with radius ε1/2), we can write the normal coordinate si as:
si = ui + px,3(u) + O(ε2)
(A.1)
for i = 1, . . . , d; and where px,k(u) is a homogeneous polynomial of degree k whose coefficients depend on
x.18
The second Lemma has two parts: the first gives an expression for the Euclidean distance ∥y −x∥2
in terms of projection coordinates, where we interpret this not as a geodesic distance on M but a literal
Euclidean distance in Rn, i.e. if ι : M →Rn is an embedding of our manifold into ambient space then for
two nearby points x, y ∈M, we mean ∥y −x∥= ∥ι(y) −ι(x)∥; the second, gives us a change of coordinates
formula for the volume form dy (which we simply write as dy) in terms of projection coordinates.
Lemma A.2 (Metric & Volume Comparison[21]). For any x ∈M, for sufficiently small ε and y ∈B(x, ε1/2),
we have:
i).

(A.2)

With Lemma A.3 in hand, we will now complete the proof of the main result:
Theorem A.1 (Expansion of Ordered Diffusion Kernels). Suppose M is a d-dimensional Riemannian
manifold embedded in Rn and with metric inherited from the standard Euclidean metric in ambient space.
Let l ∈C2(M) be an ordering function, and let Kε(x, y) be the corresponding ODK as defined in (5).
Then for any f ∈C3(M) the associated family of backward integral operators {Gε}ε>0 admits the following
asymptotic expansion as ε →0 when applied to f:
Gεf(x) = m(x)f(x) + ε (ωl(x)f(x) + m(x)Lf(x)) + ε3/2Ωl(x) + O(ε2),
(A.15)
at a point x ∈M a distance larger than εγ from ∂M, for a fixed γ ∈(0, 1/2); and where ωl(x) depends on
the manifold and the kernel Kε(x, ·) at x, and Ωl(x) depends on the manifold, the kernel Kε(x, ·) at x, and
also the test function f(x). The differential operator L is a second-order elliptic operator of the form:
Lf(x) = µ(x) · ∇f(x) + D(x)∆f(x)
(A.16)
where m(x), µ(x) and D(x), satisfy:

The associated expansion for the adjoint operator is done in the weak sense, which other shows that up
to the resolution of the L2(M) inner product, the operator A is equal to the operator B when applied to
some f ∈L2(M) ∩Dom(A) ∩Dom(B) is for any g ∈L2(M) ∩Dom(A) ∩Dom(B), we have ⟨g, Af⟩L2(M) =
⟨g, Bf⟩L2(M). To do this, we must restrict the domain of f to be in C3(M)∩L2(M), and thus the Theorem
and proof are as follows:
50

It is worth making a short practical comment about κ(x), which we introduced as a function of utility,
acting more like a hyperparameter of the kernel than a parameter needing to be inferred; we did this
because it allows one to decouple the scale of the ordering function (which has no effect on the LQO, ≤l,
that it implies) from the magnitude of the drift-term, thus separating the task of inferring direction and
magnitude cleanly. If one wishes to make the kernel entirely agnostic to the scale of l(x), then one should set
κ(x) = ∥∇l(x)∥. In the proofs of Theorem 3.1, we assumed that κ(x) was bounded away from zero, but if l(x)
is chosen arbitrarily and κ(x) = ∥∇l(x)∥, then this might not always be the case, thus creating a singularity
within the kernel. If this occurs let Mc denote a connected critical submanifold of l(x) - where we note
dim(Mc) can equal any value from 0 (an isolated critical point) to d (an entire critical region) - then there
are three possible courses of action: firstly, one could attempt to mollify l(x) into a new function ˜l(x) that
implies an identical ordering but avoids the criticality, this is only guaranteed to be possible when the critical
51


submanifold is of dimension (d−1) and l(x) is strictly monotonic on the leaves of the implied foliation of some
open region containing the critical manifold. More precisely, there must exist a d-dimensional neighbourhood
U of the (d −1)-dimensional critical submanifold, Mc, such that there exists a submersion φ : U →R with
φ−1(0) = Mc, and a smooth strictly monotonic function f : φ(U) →l(U) such that l|U = f ◦φ; the mollified
˜l(x) is then constructed as a smooth gluing of l|M\U and the submersion φ. In all other cases, one can
either enforce a uniform lower bound on κ(x) (i.e. define ˜κ(x) = max(κ(x), c) for c > 0 as small as the
data allows), this will result in a new drift-term τ(x) ∇l(x)
˜κ(x) which strictly underestimates the magnitude of
the velocity in the set {x ∈M : 0 < κ(x) < c}, but on the critical manifold - since we know the drift term
will be zero19 - the dynamics will be as desired; if dim(Mc) = d this is the only available correction. The
final course of action that can be applied if dim(Mc) < d is to simply cut out the critical manifold(s) and
treat them like boundaries of the new manifold M \ Mc, thus referring us back to the previous discussion.
Unless the critical manifold has very specific dynamical significance, and thus underestimating the dynamics
would be damaging, it is likely more practical to simply apply a uniform lower bound on κ(x) or to mollify
away the critical submanifold if possible or to change the form of κ(x) altogether. When working with real
data, in all cases except where l(x) has been constructed to have a d-dimensional critical submanifold, it is
highly unlikely that we would ever numerically compute the derivative of l(x) to be so close to zero as to
cause a singularity in our kernel. This is simply due to the fact that whenever dim(Mc) < d we will have
vol(Mc) = 0, is a set of measure zero and thus is unlikely to bother us in practice; indeed depending on how
one numerically computes ∥∇l(x)∥is may already be reasonable to clamp κ(x) between a lower and upper
value to avoid anomalous estimates skewing ones results.
A.1
Local-Ordered Diffusion Kernels
To prove the expansion result of the locally Ordered Diffusion Kernel (lODK), we only assume that the
local ordering function, l(x, y), is defined on a restricted domain in the second argument; thus, there is an
additional technical consideration that we will discuss first before completing the proof without ambiguity.
In the proof of Theorem 3.1 we take γ ∈(0, 1
2) and consider the integral of the kernel about x over M in
a ball of radius εγ. But the local ordering function at x ∈M, written lx : Ux →R : y 7→l(x, y), is only
assumed to be defined on a restricted domain; it is always possible to extend lx : Ux →R smoothly to all
of M, so the integral in Gεf outside of B(x, εγ) are still well-defined and of order O(ε2), as discussed in
the proof of Theorem A.1, but we need to make sure that the integral inside the ball is independent of this
extension.
In light of the discussion of the local ordering function given in Appendix B.1, we know that the domain
of lx is an open neighbourhood of x over which the exponential map must be a diffeomorphism; we can frame
this in terms of the injectivity radius of expx, which is defined as:
inj(x) = sup{r > 0 : expx |B(0,r) is a diffeomorphism onto its image.}
(A.55)
Thus, we can require that Ux ⊆exp−1
x (inj(x)), although we note that the exponential map will be diffeo-
morphic on a star-shaped open set and thus we are taking a restriction by requiring Ux to be a ball. We
assume in the statement of this Theorem that M has bounded geometry, which means there exists some
c > 0 such that infx∈M inj(x) = c, and thus we can take the domain of each Ux = exp−1
x (B(0, c)) and by
nature of the exponential map being an isometry we need to choose ε small enough such that ε ≤c1/γ; such
a ε guarantees that the asymptotic expansion of Gεf(x) is valid uniformly across all x. When we compute
the integral in Gεf(x) we are implicitly taking any bounded C2(M × M) extension of l(x, y), and taking
the integral over all y ∈M.
After making this consideration, we can state and prove the expansion of lODKs in much the same
manner as we have done previously:
Theorem A.3 (Expansion of locally Ordered Diffusion Kernels). Let M be a d-dimensional Riemannian
manifold with bounded geometry embedded in Rn, and with the metric inherited from the standard Euclidean
metric. Let l(x, y) be a C2(Dom(l)) local ordering function of two inputs and let b(x) = ∇lx(x). For the
19The only critical manifold in which the ordering function l(x) implies a direction is the aforementioned monotonic (d −1)-
dimensional critical submanifold case.

In the definition of an anisotropic ODK, we have the term (y −x)T A(x)−1(y −x), where A is a symmetric,
uniformly elliptic

suppose ι : M →Rn is our isotropic embedding of M — for now, let us denote for any x ∈M, ˜x = ι(x);
before we stated Lemma A.3 we noted that ∥y−x∥:= ∥˜y−˜x∥, which is sloppy notation but doesn’t cause any
issues. However, in an anODK, for a fixed x ∈M, and denoting B = A(x)−1 ∈Rd×d, then (˜y −˜x)T B(˜y −˜x)
does not make any sense as the dimensions are incompatible. So we either have to view B as being in Rn×n,
or y −x ∈Rd, if we suppose that A really is a (2, 0)-tensor field on M, which would make B a

(A.68)
Since we construct the normal coordinates and projection coordinates with respect to the same orthonormal
basis, we will have DιT Dι = Id by definition of the isometry, which makes the columns of Dι(x) ∈Rn×d the
orthonormal basis of the affine tangent space T˜xι(M) ⊂Rn, and thus the coordinate chart of the projection
coordinates around x will equal ϕx : U →Rd : y 7→Dι(x)T (ι(y) −ι(x)). Therefore, assuming B(x) has
been written in projection coordinates, equation (A.68) will equal uT A−1u exactly. We can also view this
equation as before with ˜y −˜x unchanged and with the symmetric quadratic form being with respect to
˜B(x) := Dι(x)B(x)Dι(x)T ∈Rn×n, where naturally ˜B will be a rank-d n × n matrix.
The more complex case arises if we don’t consider ˜B(x) to be a projection onto the tangent space, i.e.
we view y −x as ι(y) −ι(x) and ˜B(x) ∈Rn×n is a symmetric uniformly elliptic matrix with rank greater
than d. Theoretically, we can avoid such cases by restricting to the tangent space, but in practice, one might
encounter a situation such as this if one constructs ˜B from the local covariance structure of the embedded
data D ⊂Rn. To handle this we will have to establish the following notation: for ι : M →ι(M) =: ˜

A.2.1
Viewing A(x) as a non-trivial Riemannian Metric
At the beginning of section 3.2 we explained that in definition 3.4 we split the diffusion, D(x) = 1
2ρ(x)2A(x),
into terms involving ρ(x)2 and A(x) so that we can discriminate between viewing A(x) as a diffusion and a
non-trivial Riemannian metric — we will now consider A(x) to be as in this latter case. The Riemannian
metric is a

from equation (34) that A−1(x) acts like a metric on M, thus we set (g(x))ij = (A−1(x))ij — we will use
the convention that gij equals the (i, j)th element of the inverse metric and since g = A−1 we will denote
gij = Aij. The infinitesimal generator of an Itˆo SDE with gradient-flow drift given by µ(x) = τ(x)

Reconstructing the Laplace-Beltrami operator with respect to a non-trivial Riemannian metric was consider
by Berry & Sauer in Local Kernels [11], where they navigated this same issue by defining a symmetrised
local kernel ¯Kε(x, y) = Kε(x, y) + Kε(y, x) whose Laplacian approximates the Laplace-Beltrami operator
with respect to some metric ˜g under the assumptions that D = {xi}m
i=1 can been sampled according to the

construction is that they lose all other advection terms. We proceed by rewriting the missing advection term

that µeff(x) = a(x)∇φ(x), let ω = µ♭
eff ∈T ∗M be this vector-fields associated 1-form, then we know that
dω = da ∧dω, and therefore we know that ω ∧dω = 0 (since adφ ∧da ∧dφ ≡0), but the converse is not
necessarily true. The intuition behind this constraint (that one might notice is the Frobenius integrability
condition) is that for any x ∈M where ω̸ = 0, then Bx{v ∈TxM : ωx(v) = 0} defines a (d −1)-dimensional
subspace of TxM, we can stich this subspaces together to get (d −1)-dimensional submanifolds of M, very
much like the level sets of an ordering function. The differential dω, effectively measures how close ω is to
being (locally) generated by the gradient of a scalar function — we can see this from the previous argument
that ω = adφ
=⇒
ω ∧dω = 0 — strictly, dω is measuring the circulations of ω around x (i.e.
the
rotational dynamics). The condition ω ∧dω = 0 is weaker than ω = adφ, because it allows for singularities
when µeff(x) = 0, just as in our discussion of handling poorly conditioned ordering functions earlier. Thus,
quite poetically, for us to represent the infinitesimal generator of the Itˆo SDE dxt = τ
κ∇l dt + ρdwt on a
Riemannian manifold with non-trivial metric (M, A−1) as given in equation (A.104), and where l(x) is an
ordering function (almost) foliating the manifold, then the effective drift implied by the metric A−1 in the
form of the Laplace-Beltrami operator must also (almost) foliate the manifold into sets perpendicular to its
advection.

section 3.2.1. The condition that div(A)(x) ≡0 makes A into a stress-tensor field and roughly means that
there are no sources of sinks of weight in the coordinate functions of A; effectively, A(x) is the diffusion
equivalent of a gradient-flow drift, but in a more structure way than if we only have ω ∧dω = 0. In all of
what follows, we assume that σ2

is the best fit to the data; furthermore, given we assume our data is generated by some dynamical process, it is
likely true that the covariance that best fits the data and the underlying covariance σ(x)σ(x)T will be highly
correlated at any x ∈M. We also add that if A(x) = ρ2Id, then |A(x)|−1/2 = ρ−d, so if ρ(x) ∝∥x −xk∥
where xk is the kth closest sample in D to any x ∈M, then by the simplest possible approximation to
the sampling density we would already have q(x) ∝∥x −xk∥−d [60]; having q(x) ∝|A(x)|−1/2 is thus the
simplest anisotropic generalisation of this result.
In a similar vein to the previous reasoning we could define an stODK, as described in section 3.2.1,
with ρ1(x) ∝|A(x)|η/2ρ2 and ρ2(y) ∝|A(y)|−η/2 with proportionality such that ρ(x)−2 [ρ1(x)ρ2(x)] =

proportional to the volume form and, and thus we are biasing rescaling the terms of the ODK inside h(·)
by a factor proportional to volA−1(x)/volA−1(y), thus biasing towards transitioning to higher volume (or
lower density if q(x) ∝|A(x)|−1/2) states; in addition ρ1 ∝ρd+2
A
which fits nicely with the factor of (d + 2)
in the self-tuning bandwidth formulation of ODK, recalling that this factor initially comes from making a
substitution within an integral and thus it makes sense why taking ρ2 to be a volume form would recover
this. If we take η = −1/d, then ρ1 ∝ρA and ρ2 ∝ρA, which is a much more familiar symmetric bandwidth
formulation (i.e. β = 1/2 regularisation in section 3.2.1), thus with the correct constants of proportionality
we can also use stODKs to recapture the Laplace-Beltrami operator when ∂iAij ≡0.
To summarise, we have extended ODKs to anisotropic diffusions at the cost of coupling the drift and
diffusion in the infinitesimal generator; analogous to how TMDmap kernels couple drift and diffusion un-
avoidably. We then proposed to approximate the uncoupled anisotropic transition operator by using the
Strang Splitting scheme, and as a result we are capable (especially upon combining anODKs with lODKs)
59


of approximating the infinitesimal generator of an arbitrary Itˆo process. We then discussed how one can
interpret the anisotropic covariance matrix A(x) as a non-trivial Riemannian metric; in this scenario, the
coupling we observed between the first- and second-order terms now works in our favour, but the second-order
term only captures the trace of the Hessian and thus does not approximate the Laplace-Beltrami operator
with respect to A−1. We showed that under some mild constraints on A−1, namely that the effective drift
it implies is capable of (almost) foliating M with its tranverse submanifolds; this is an interesting condition
as it is effectively identical to the discussion we had in section 2.2 about what makes l(x) a good ordering
function. Under this constraint, we show that one can reconstruct the full infinitesimal generator (advection
term and Laplace-Beltrami term) by applying multi-Ordered Diffusion Kernels discussed in section 3.1.1;
α-regularised ODKs as discussed in section 3.3; and self-tuning ODKs as discussed in the next section. Using
an mODK makes the effective drift very real from the perspective of the transition probabilities, which is
a technical inaccuracy but is easy to apply; α-normalised ODKs are the most satisfying method but they
require that the sampling distribution q(x) is uniform with respect to the volume form |A|−1/2; stODKs
reconstruct the effective drift in a truly effective manner, and do so with no extra constraints, however one
does need to be careful that proportionality constants satisfy η(d + 2)ρ1ρ2 = ρ2.
A.2.2
Self-Tuning Ordered Diffusion Kernels
In this appendix, we will provide the proof for the expansion of self-tuning Ordered Diffusion Kernels
(stODKs) as defined in section 3.2.1. All the difficulty in this proof is making a detailed accounting of all
the new integral terms that occur once you expand ρ2(y) about ρ2(x); this task can be greatly simplified
through the following Lemma:

A.3
Density Regularised ODKs
In the literature, particularly in Diffusion Maps [21], they introduce an α ∈[0, 1] parameter to tune
the strength of density regularisation, with α = 0 being no density regularisation, α = 1/2 being their
Fokker-Planck normalisation, and α = 1 being fully regularised. This amounts to defining Kε,α(x, y) :=
Kε(x, y)q(y)−α and only makes sense if we change the integrals for the backward and forward integral op-
erators to be with respect to the underlying sampling measure q(x). In other words, we now consider the

We see that if one doesn’t fully regularise for the sampling density, then there will be another advection
term proportional to ∇q that is weighted by a factor of (1 −α). If one progressively increases α from 0
to 1, then the contribution of this additional term decreases. If one wants an exact reconstruction of the
underlying dynamics then one should always consider the α = 1 case; but if one’s data is highly noisy (many
outliers) then pushing the effective drift towards the core concentration of the data may be wise depending
on your ultimate goal. The α-normalised adjoint has an expansion analogous to the previous results given
in Theorem 3.2.
A.4

in probability of the discrete operators to the continuous operators. In what follows, we will use the Bernstein
inequality as given below:

B
Supplementary Notes
B.1
Ordering Functions
In this supplementary note, we seek to clarify what is meant by an Ordering Function. It is important to say
that an ordering function, as we conceptualise it, is not really something that can be defined independently
based on some distinguished set of properties, and is more like a representation of some mathematical
ordering of elements (i.e. a notion of something being larger or smaller, or happening later or earlier, than
something else)
Orderings of mathematical spaces are studied in order theory, and many deep results come from this
field; however, there are many different axiomatic constructions to choose from when defining an ordering
([96, 54, 43]). The idea of an ordering function is not novel in principle, since ultimately what an ordering
function does is try to map the natural ordering of the real numbers onto some abstract mathematical
space; in the literature, concepts like our ordering function are referred to as the utility functions or utility
representations [91, 68, 31, 16]. We will give a discussion of how an ordering function fits within the more well-
studied field of representations reflexive and transitive quasi-orders, sometimes called pre-orders, although
we note that it may be more appropriate to consider a more specialised construction such as d-spaces [38],
which consider orderings defined by a set of directed paths, or streams [54], which considers a circulation
that associates a quasi-order to the open sets of a topological space, both of which generalise quasi-orders.
Definition B.1 (Quasi/Pre-Order). Let X be some set. A binary relation ≤: X × X →{0, 1} is called a
quasi-order if it satisfies the following two conditions:
i). (Reflexivity): ∀x ∈X, x ≤x
ii). (Transitivity): ∀x, y, z ∈X, (x ≤y) ∧(y ≤z) =⇒(x ≤z)
If, in addition, the quasi-order satisfies the following linearity (totality) condition, we call ≤a linear (total)
quasi-order (LQO):
iii). (Linearity): ∀x, y ∈X, (x ≤y) ∨(y ≤x)
Let ≤be a quasi-order: then if x ≤y or y ≤x, we say that x and y are comparable — a Linear
Quasi-Order (LQO) is thus a quasi-order where all elements of X are comparable; we can define a strict
quasi-order, <, as a restriction of ≤, through x < y
⇐⇒
x ≤y ∧y ≰x; we can define an equivalence
relation x ≃y ⇐⇒x ≤y ∧y ≤x, which we call (quasi-)equality; for two subsets U, V ⊂X, if for every
x ∈U and y ∈V we have x ≤y (x < y or x ≃y), then we write U ≤V (U < V or U ≃V ); for any A ⊂X
we can define the lower, equal and upper sets as:
L(A) = {x ∈X|∃y ∈A : x ≤y},
U(A) = {x ∈X|∃y ∈A : y ≤x},
E(A) = {x ∈X|∃y ∈A : x ≃y}
(B.1)
A quasi-order is called spacious if for every x, y ∈X such that x ≤y, then L(x) ⊆L(y) (if you are given
a strict quasi-order this condition would be cl(L(x)) ⊆L(y) and it is true that the strict restriction of a
quasi-order satisfying the former property, will also satisfy the latter property. A strict quasi-order is said
to be separable if for any x, y ∈X such that x < y, there will exist a p ∈X such that x < p < y, likewise
≤is separable if its strict restriction is separable; if X is a topological space that is itself separable, and
≤is continuous (see definition B.2 below) then < will be separable. Assume from now on that X is a
topological space, currently a quasi-order on X will be entirely independent of the topology on X, but we
may connect them with notions of continuity that mirror T1 and T2 (Hausdorff) spaces: effectively, if ≤
is lower-(upper-)semicontinuous then for all x, y ∈X such that x ≰y (y ≰x) there exists an open set of
U ⊂X containing a such that U ≰b (b ≰U); a result of this is that if ≤is lower (upper) semicontinuous
then its lower (upper) sets will be topologically closed. A quasi-order is called semicontinuous if it is both
upper- and lower-semicontinuous. A quasi-order is said to be continuous if:
Definition B.2. A quasi-order, ≤, of a topological space X, is said to be continuous if ∀x, y ∈X such that
x ≰y, there exists open neighbourhoods U and V of x and y, respectively, such that U ∩V = ∅and ∀u ∈U
and ∀v ∈V then u ≰v.
It is true that continuity implies semicontinuity, and indeed, if ≤is linear, semicontinuity and continuity
are equivalent [96]. For a continuous quasi-order, the lower and upper sets will be topologically closed, and
66


furthermore, the lower and upper sets of the strict restriction will be open; a continuous quasi-order will
have a closed graph in the product topology of X × X, whereas semicontinuity does not guarantee this. For
what follows, we will always assume that ≤is continuous.
The notion of an order we have built so far is very abstract and rather difficult to study and visualise, but
we realise that for any reasonable ordering we might consider we are effectively mapping the only canonical
ordering we have, that is the ordering of the real numbers, onto local regions of X. In other words, under
mild assumptions on X and ≤, we have for every x ∈X a neighbourhood U of x and an order-preserving (i.e.
strictly increasing/isotone) map f : U →R; the question becomes when will these local functions be nice (i.e.
continuous) and can we glue these local f’s together to form a global continuous function f : (X, ≤) →(R, ≤)
that satisfies:
∀x, y ∈X : x < y =⇒f(x) < f(y)
(B.2)
where we must use the strict relation, as otherwise any constant f would be satisfactory. In the literature,
these functions are referred to as utility functions, and more broadly they form a utility representation of
quasi-ordered topological spaces.20 We note that in the representation given in equation (B.2), f does not
entirely determine/characterise the ordering since the implication only goes one way; however, generalising
this to an if and only if statement would immediately imply that ≤is linear, which is a strong condi-
tion. This motivated the development of so-called multi-utility representations of quasi-orders, in which
we take a (potentially uncountable) family of functions F = {fi}i∈I such that ∀x, y ∈X : x ≤y
=⇒
(f(x) ≤f(y) for all f ∈F ). Thus, the family F entirely characterises the ordering, which feels more useful;
there is still the issue of existence that we tackle in the following proposition:
Proposition B.1. Let (X, ≤) be a continuous quasi-ordered topological space. The following will hold:
(i). If the associated strict quasi-order, <, is separable and spacious, then there exists a continuous utility
function, f : X →R, such that x ≤y =⇒f(x) ≤f(y), for (X, ≤).
(ii). If the associated strict quasi-order, <, is separable and spacious, then there exists a continuous utility
function, f : X →R, such that x ≤y ⇐⇒f(x) ≤f(y) if and only if ≤is linear.
(iii). If X is a locally-compact Hausdorff space that is also σ-compact,21 then there always exists a continuous
multi-utility representation of (X, ≤). If in addition X is second-countable ( ⇐⇒C(X) is separable),
then this representation can be taken to be countable.
Proof. (i) This is a known result of Peleg (Theorem 3.1 of [68]) that shows for a separable and spacious,
continuous strict quasi-order, <, there will exist a continuous utility function, f : X →R, in the form
x < y =⇒f(x) < f(y). If we first take the quotient of X by the equality relation, ≃, then we can define
the relation ⪯, on X/ ≃through: [x] ⪯[y] ⇐⇒∃˜x ∈[x], ˜y ∈[y] : ˜x ≤˜y. This relation will be well-defined,
reflexive and transitive and thus a quasi-order; furthermore, if < is separable and spacious, so too will ≺.
Therefore by the Theorem of Peleg, there will exist ˜f : (X/ ≃) →R such that [x] ≺[y] =⇒˜f([x]) < ˜f([y])
that is constant on the equal sets of (X, ≤) (i.e. if x ≃y then f([x]) = f([y])). Thus, by properties of
quotient spaces, there will be a continuous function f : X →R such that x < y
=⇒
f(x) < f(y) and
x ≃y =⇒f(x) = f(y).
(ii) The forward implication is trivial, and (i) shows that there exists a continuous f such that x < y =⇒
f(x) < f(y); if x ≃y, then f(x) = f(y), if x < y then f(x) < f(y), and if y < x f(y) < f(x), thus f entirely
characterises ≤and by linearity we are done.
(iii) This is a known result and derives from a result of Evren & Ok (see Theorem 1 of [31]), which states
the existence of a continuous multi-utility representation. The countability of this representation comes from
locally compact Hausdorff spaces that are also σ-compact and second-countable, having a countable basis
that makes C(X) separable.
There are several takeaways here: first, utility representations are abundant for nice quasi-orders (contin-
uous, separable and spacious) defined on nice topological spaces (effectively, we want X to be metrisable, and
we note that second-countable, locally compact Hausdorff spaces that are σ-compact are always metrisable).
20More precisely, this is a Richter-Peleg representation.
21A topological space X is Hausdorff if any two points are jointly distinguished by disjoint open sets; X is locally compact if
around every point there exists an open set whose closure is compact; a locally compact space is σ-compact if it can be written
as a countable union of these compact sets.
67


Thus, if we are focused on continuous linear quasi-orders, we may as well consider the function l : X →R,
that generates the LQO, ≤l, through:
x ≤l y ⇐⇒l(x) ≤l(y)
(B.3)
as being the fundamental object of study, and thus we call this an ordering function (although, as we
mentioned at the beginning, it doesn’t seem correct to define the ordering function outright); we could also
view the multiple-Ordered Diffusion Kernels (mODKs) of section 3.1.1 as being analogous to multi-utility
representations. If we now restrict ourselves to defining ordering functions on a d-dimensional topological
manifold, i.e. l : M →R, then we see that if dlx̸ = 0 for any x in an open set U ⊂M, then the level sets of l
form a codimension-one foliation of U, i.e. we have sliced U along the level sets of l, which are equivalently
the equal sets of the order ≤l, and this slicing is such that each level set is a (d−1)-dimensional submanifold.
We can then view the second term of the ODK:
Kε(x, y) = h
∥y −x∥2
ερ(x)2
+ (l(y) −l(x) −ετ(x)κ(x))2
ερ(x)2κ(x)2
−(l(y) −l(x))2
ερ(x)2κ(x)2

(B.4)
as an advection across the leaves of the foliation, and the first term as a diffusion applied after this initial
advection.
By Theorem 3.1, we see that the first-order term of the expansion of Kε is proportional to
∇l, thus, (spacious and separable) continuous linear quasi-orders (whose equal sets are sufficiently smooth)
defined on differentiable manifolds is entirely characterised by a gradient flow; we could say that the order
is generated by this flow. Thus these C2(M) ordering functions characterise all sufficiently smooth linear
quasi-orders; this motivates us to think of an ordering as some representation of a vector field, and thus
the existence of a suitable ordering function depends on some notion of integrability of this vector field.
Furthermore, if we allow for our vector fields to be more general than gradient-flows, then statement (ii) in
proposition B.1, gives us another characterisation relation (an if and only if statement) between orderings
and multi-utility representations, thus taking an mODK with a finite (k = min(2d, n)) number of ordering
functions would allow us to represent arbitrary vector-field proportional to Pk
i=1 τi(x)∇li(x), thus allowing
us to generalise beyond (sufficiently smooth) linear quasi-orders to (sufficient smooth) non-linear (partial)
quasi-orders.
However, if we are now trying to view an ordering that is both nice and as generally applicable as possible,
then we notice that (global) transitivity limits us from representing cyclic orders. As an example, suppose
we wished to order the hour hand of a clock: clearly 1 < 2, but we also have that 2 < 3, and 3 < 4, and so
on, until we have 12 < 1; by transitivity, this implies 2 < 1 and thus our ordering is contradictory. To order
the clock, or more generally to order cyclic domains, we need to relax the assumption of transitivity; there
have been several formulations of such an idea of locally ordered topological spaces, these include: a very
intuitive formulation in terms of open sets through local partial orders, whereby partial we mean that ≤is
also antisymmetric22, such a space is called a local pospace (see [33] for more details); a simple removal of
transitivity, where ≤is simply reflexive and its graph, {(x, y) ∈X ×X : x ≤y}, is closed with respect to the
product topology, we call such spaces weakly ordered topological spaces (or wospaces); a characterisation of
an ordering based on a set of distinguished paths, dX = {γ : I →X}, that satisfies all constant path are in
dX, dX is closed under reparametisation of the interval I by weakly increasing functions, and dX is closed
under concaternation of consecutive paths in dX, thus we can define a relation x ≤y if there is a path in dX
originating at x and ending at y, we call such spaces d-spaces (see [38] for more details); a characterisation
of an ordering based on a circulation that is effectively a pre-sheaf, i.e. we can restrict the circulation to a
preorder on any open set, we call such an ordering a stream (see [54] for more details).
All of the above methods (other than weakly ordered spaces) will generalise easily to cyclic orders; the
study of local pospaces naturally includes the study of global pospaces (which is similar to the discussion
we give above), while d-spaces and streams naturally can represent any continuous quasi-ordered topological
space — therefore, these last two constructions are most aligned with the generalisation we seek to make.
Just as the discussion of continuous quasi-orders defined on a d-dimensional manifold led to a discussion of
vector fields and integrability, so too will the notion of a local continuous quasi-order lead to a discussion of
locally defined vector fields and their local integrability. Furthermore, since if our construction allows us to
22Where antisymmetry is taken to mean ∀x, y ∈X if x ≤y ∧y ≤x =⇒x = y where equality is of elements not of orders,
i.e. not as with ≃as we have had with quasi-orders.
68


say that a subset U ⊂X has a local ordering ≤U, then any restriction of this ordering to V ⊂U will be a
local ordering on V , which fits nicely into the description of sheafs and thus functors between categories, local
orderings are often studied in this light; intrestingly, it was proven by Haucourt that streams and d-spaces
are almost isomorphic in a category theoretic sense [43].
In this paper we will not generalise an ordering function (or utility representation) to these more general
notions of an ordering, but will rather define a simple construction — that mirrors the definition of local
pospaces given in [33] but differs in some important ways — that fits naturally within the framework of a
local ordering function, l(x, y), as introduced in section 3.1.
We define a Locally Linear Quasi-Ordered Topological Space (LLQOTS) as follows:
Definition B.3 (LLQOTS). A Locally Linear Quasi-Ordered Topological Space (LLQOTS) is a pair (X, OX),
where X is a topological space and OX = {(U, ≤U)} is called the order set of X, and is composed of open
linearly ordered subsets of X satisfying the following properties:
1). Each U ∈OX is open, connected and has compact closure.
2). Every ≤U is a reflexive, transitive and linear binary relation on U that is continuous and separable
with respect to the subspace topology.
3). There is no element U ∈OX that is strictly contained in another.
4). For every (U, ≤U), (V, ≤V ) ∈OX, the orderings will agree on their intersection.
5). There are no extremal (maximal or minimal) elements in any (U, ≤U) ∈OX.
Let us denote by Xr = S
U∈OX U the regular set of the order (or the regularly ordered set), and let Xc = X\Xr
be the critical set of the order. If cl(X) = cl(Xr), then we say the local ordering is complete; if, in addition,
cl(X) = S
U∈OX cl(U), we say that OX is locally complete.
We note that if OX is complete and (Xr, ≤Xr) ∈OX, then by assumption (4) we will have returned
to the case of continuous LQOs on X. This construction is very similar to how local pospaces are defined,
except we do not require partiality and we do not assume that S
U∈OX U = X — this relaxation is important
when introducing local ordering functions (which we could reasonably call local utility-representations). To
help our intuition we could imagine that we are somewhere on Earth looking out into the distance, our eyes
will only allow us to see so far before the curvature of earth breaks our line of sight, or a mountain blocks
the view of something behind it; the locally ordered neighbourhoods encode that we can only see so far, and
the peaks of mountains would be contained in the closures of these neighbourhoods.
We do not develop the theory of LLQOTs fully in this paper as it does not seem suitable, but we will
make the following general claims: the restriction to local orderings allows us to represent cyclic orders (this
can be seen in the numerical example given in section 4.1); formalising the idea of a maximal extension23 of
a connected ordered set will allow us to study the topology of X through the criticalities (the parts we have
to cut out in order to define an LQO) of the ordering, and furthermore, the set of all maximal extensions
of a set can itself be ordered;24 the elements of X that are not contained in S
U∈OX U will conveniently
be the local maximal and minimal elements of the local order, which as we recall from the discussion of
criticalities of the ordering function (i.e. x ∈M such that ∇l(x) = 0), will allow us to assume the local
ordering functions form proper foliations of each U ∈OX, thus making it easier to discuss and characterise
a local ordering function.
By the assumption (2) above and statement (ii) in proposition B.1, we can associate a continuous function
fU to every (U, ≤U) ∈OX, and by assumption (1) and (5) we know that fU does not have any extremal
values in U, but does take both a maximal and minimal value on ∂U (the boundary of U, defined as ∂U =
cl(U) \ int(U)), since these utility representations are invariant under composition with strictly increasing
functions (i.e. if φ : fU(U) →R is strictly increasing then φ ◦fU is also a utility function), we can assume
that fU : U →I = (0, 1). Assumption (4) implies for every (U, ≤U), (V, ≤V ) ∈OX there exists a strictly
increasing continuous bijection φV
U : fU(U ∩V ) →fV (U ∩V ) such that fV = φV
U ◦fU.
Assume now that X is Hausdorff and OX is locally complete, i.e. every point x is in the closure of at
least one of the ordered sets. We seem to construct a local ordering function (local utility-representation),
23An extension, (W, ≤W ), over OX is any open connected ordered subset of X, s.t. W is covered by sets in OX, and ≤W
agrees with ≤U on W ∩U for any U ∈OX. A maximal extension is an extension that is not strictly contained in any other
extension.
24Furthermore, if we formalise a notion of path-extensions it is likely possible to make a direct connection to d-spaces, as
described in [38].
69


f(x, y), that satisfies x ≤Ux y
⇐⇒
f(x, y) ≥0, where Ux is some open neighbourhood of x. Following
from the previous discussion, we see that the only difficulty is defining the open sets Ux for elements of Mc;
suppose x ∈∂U for some U ∈OX is not contained in any other element of OX, then this can break down
in two distinct situations: the closure (with sequences) of the order, ≤U on cl(U), forms a cyclic ordering;
there is another (V, ≤V ) ∈OX such that x ∈∂V and the closure of the ordering ≤U does not agree with
the closure of the ordering ≤V . In the latter case we can either define the set Ux = (U ∪V ) ∪(∂U ∩∂V )
and allow for fx(y) := f(x, y) to be discontinuous or we must assume the stronger version of assumption (4)
that being the transverse closure by sequences of any (U, ≤U), (V, ≤V ) ∈OX must agree on intersection —
we could call this a transversly closed local ordering.
Resolving the cyclic order is more technical: for this to occur x must be both a maximal and minimal
element of U (since cl(U) is compact), thus we must take an equal set, E, in U that is strictly less (equivalently
strictly greater) than x, letting Ex = [x] ∩U denote the equal set of x in the closure of U, we can and define
Ux = (U ∪Ex)\E, this will be an open subset of X that is linearly orderable given OX. The difficulty comes
from choosing which E to cut out and is effectively equivalent to choosing which point on a circle to cut out
to make S1 \ {p} ∼= (0, 1), to do this unambiguously you would need, at least, that X be metrisable so that
you can take E such that it is in the midpoint of U. For all other points of closure we can construct Ux as
follows: if x ∈∂X, then simply taking Ux = cl(U) is satisfactory (since this will be open in the topology
of X); if x is a transverse or extremal point of closure, we could take either Ux = (U ∪V ) ∪(∂U ∩∂V ), or
Ux = int(U ↑∪V ↓) where U ↑is the upward closure (closure in terms of increasing sequences) and V ↓is the
downward closure (closure in terms of decreasing sequences), respectively.
Therefore, if X is Hausdorff, and OX is locally complete and transversely closed, then for every x ∈X
we can construct an open set Ux containing x such that OX implies a continuous LQO on Ux. By statement
(ii) of proposition B.1 there will exist a continuous fx for every such neighbourhood; thus on the domain
D = F
x∈X Ux, we can construct a local ordering function as f : D →R : (x, y) 7→fx(y) and which satisfies
x ≤Ux y ⇐⇒f(x, y) ≥0. We note that the D that we construct is not necessarily the best (i.e. largest)
domain possible; to construct a maximal domain for f we would need each Ux to be the maximal extension
over OX containing x; since we do not formally introduce this idea in this paper, we will end that discussion
here. In the above discussion, we have proven the following proposition:
Proposition B.2. Let X be a Hausdorff topological space, and let OX be a locally complete and trans-
versely closed continuous local linear quasi-order. Then there always exists a local utility-representation,
f : Dom(f) →R of OX, where Dom(f) = F
x∈X Ux for open neighbourhoods Ux of x satisfying for any
x ∈X and y ∈Ux, x ≤Ux y
⇐⇒
f(x, y) ≥0. If, in addition, X is metrisable (e.g. locally compact,
second-countable, and σ-compact) and the maximal extensions of OX are not arbitrarily small, then each Ux
can be chosen to be not arbitrarily small.
Proof. The first statement has been shown by construction in the previous discussion. If X is metrisable
then it is homeomorphic to a metric space (M, d), let us denote this homeomorphism as ϕ : (X, τ) →(M, d).
Therefore, not arbitrarily small means there is a uniform lower bound on the diameter of the sets ϕ(Ux) ⊂M
with respect to d. As mentioned, we can construct Ux to be a maximal extension over OX containing x, and
by assumption of the proposition, the maximal extensions will not be arbitrarily small, so we are done.
A local ordering function allows us to unify Richter-Peleg utility functions for continuous LQOs, and
multi-utility representations for non-linear (incomplete) quasi-orders:
Utility Function:
f(x, y) = f(y) −f(x)
(B.5)
Multi-Utility Representations:
f(x, y) = inf
i∈I [fi(y) −fi(x)]
(B.6)
where f : X →R is a Richter-Peleg utility representation, and {fi}i∈I is a multi-utility representation.
Trivially in both cases f(x, x) = 0 and by the definition of the utility representations, we will have x ≤
y ⇐⇒f(x, y) ≥0. Furthermore, can represent the more complex notion of continuous LLQOs and even
local pospaces naturally within the local ordering function.
If we restrict ourselves to smooth d-dimensional Riemannian manifolds, which we denote by M, then
we can frame a local ordering function in terms of vector fields again. We note that it is true that any
arbitrary smooth vector field b : M →TM can be written as b(x) = ∇lx(x) for some smooth scalar function
70


lx : M →R. We can see this by simply writing l(x, y) = gx(exp−1
x (y), b(x)) where expx : ˜U →U where
U is an open neighbourhood of x, and ˜U is an open ball of radius inj(x) centred at the origin of Rd; the
exponential map defines the normal coordinates about x and in these normal coordinates gx = Id and
therefore l(x, y) = yibi(x), it is then trivial true that ∇lx(x) = b(x). The assumption of bounded geometry
simply allows us to bound the injectivity radius away from zero, i.e. there exists some c > 0 such that
infx∈M inj(x) = c; thus, ˜l : M × B(0, c) →R : (x, u) →l(x, expx(u)) is a well-defined smooth function.
The condition x ≤y ⇐⇒l(x, y) ≥0 is equivalent to y −x being in the same half-space as b(x), which is
simply a measure of directionality - exactly as we desire for our ordering function to imply. Furthermore, if
ω : M →T ∗M was the dual covector field of b : M →TM, then by Frobenius’ Theorem, we know that
the kernel of ω will generate a foliation if and only if ω ∧dω = 0. This equivalence allows us to construct
an ordering function from any codimension-one transversally orientable25 foliation as opposed to a foliation
defined from an ordering.
Assume that OM is locally complete, and let lU be an ordering function for some U ∈OM, then we will
have an atlas of codimension-one foliated charts that cover Mr and whose foliations agree on their inter-
sections, thus Mr admits a global codimension-one foliation, and this is generated by a nowhere vanishing
1-form ω satisfying ωU = hUdfU — this follows from the fact that transversal orientability being immediate

To summarise, if M is a smooth d-dimensional Riemannian manifold, and OM is an order set that is
locally complete and transversely closed, then the regular set will admit a codimension-one foliation with
leaves being the equal contours of OM. Furthermore, OM being locally complete implies Mc ⊆∂Mr which
can be at most a (d −1)-dimensional submanifold which means volg(Mc) = 0, i.e. almost everything is
foliated. By proposition B.2 there will always exist a local ordering function, l(x, y), and by the previous
discussion there will exist a smooth 1-form, ω : M →T ∗M such that l(x, y) = ω(x)(exp−1
x (y)) that satisfies
ω̸ = 0 and ω ∧dω = 0 on Mr. Although we resisted providing a definition of an ordering function, if we
were forced to give one, it would be:
Definition B.4 (Ordering Functions). Let M be a d-dimensional Riemannian manifold with bounded ge-
ometry, and let ω : M →T ∗M be a C2 1-form satisfying ω ∧dω = 0 and supp(ω) := cl({x ∈M : ω(x)̸ =
0}) = M. Then we call the function l : Dom(l) →R : (x, y) 7→ω(x)(exp−1
x (y)) a local ordering function,
where Dom(l) = F
x∈M Ux is such that for any x ∈M the exponential map, exp−1
x , is a diffeomorphism on
Ux. If, in addition, there exists a scalar function ˜l ∈C2(M) such that l(x, y) = ˜l(y) −˜l(x) (equivalently
ω = d˜l) then we call ˜l(x) a global ordering function.
From this definition, we know that a local ordering function defines transversally orientable codimension-
one foliations almost everywhere, i.e. the leaves of the foliation have a well-defined normal vector.27 Our
restriction of the domain ensures that l(x, y) is well-defined, and the assumption of bounded geometry ensures
this domain is not arbitrarily small, and trivially we have that l(x, x) = ω(x)(0) = 0 for every x ∈M. We
also make the important practical note that the restriction of M to be such that it admits a transversely
orientable codimension-one foliation is not at all limiting since we are assuming the manifold is generated
by some stochastic process, i.e. there really will be a well-defined normal vector for every leaf.
25A codimension-one foliation is said to be transversally orientable if the normal vector to the leaves can be chosen to have
a globally consistent unit normal vector field.
26This condition relates back to our discussion about the continuity of a local ordering function in the first argument given
in section 3.1.
27We know this because the foliation is represented by a nowhere-vanishing 1-form.

B.2
Estimating ∥∇l(x)∥on Discrete Samples
In our discussion of the form of the order regularisation function κ(x), we proposed setting κ(x) = ∥∇l(x)∥
to make ODK agnostic to the scale of l(x). In practice, it is unlikely that you will have an analytic expression
for l(x), so one must compute ∥∇l(x)∥numerically from evaluations of l(x) on the data D. There are many
methods that one could use towards this end, but we find that one in particular feels very canonical given
our assumption that the data clusters on or near an d-dimensional Riemannian submanifold M of Rn. Let
us first set up the problem: the gradient of a function f : Rn →R at a point x ∈Rn, denoted ∇f(x), is the
unique vector satisfying:
lim
y→x
f(y) −f(x) −∇f(x)T (y −x)
∥y −x∥
= 0
(B.8)
In other words, ∇f(x) defines the best possible linear approximation of f around x. For some y ∈B(x, η(x)),
where η(x) is a sufficiently small radius such that this linear approximation is reasonable, we can ignore the
limit in equation (B.8) and rearrange to find:
f(y) = f(x) + ∇f(x)T (y −x)
(B.9)
Let D = {xi}m
i=1 ⊂Rn be a finite sample of points on M, for every i = 1, . . . , m, let Ni = (B(xi, ηi)∩D)\{xi},
where again ηi is some sufficiently small radius. Then we can see that equation (B.9) defines a linear regression
problem; more precisely, it defines the problem δif = XT
i βi, where βi denotes our approximation to ∇f(xi),
and (δif)j = f(xj) −f(xi) and (Xi):,j = xj −xi, for xj ∈Ni — we are assuming for convenience that these
samples are indexed from j = 1, . . . , |Ni| in Ni. If our goal is to minimise the squared residuals, i.e. we wish
to minimise R2(βi) = ∥δif −XT
i βi∥2, then this is a least squares problem and if |Ni| ≫1 will have a unique
solution given by:
βi = (XiXT
i )−1Xiδif
(B.10)
However, if |Ni| < n — which would often be the case if we are choosing a small neighbourhood Ni of xi
and n ≫1 — then the system will be underdetermined and there will be infinitely many solutions that
minimise the squared residuals. To break this tie, the usual method is to take the solution that minimises
R2(βi) + ∥βi∥2, which will select the solution of smallest magnitude that minimises R2(βi). This can also be
expressed exactly through [61]:
βi = Xi(XT
i Xi)−1δif
(B.11)
It turns out that both of these expressions are equivalent if written in terms of components of the Singular
Value Decomposition (SVD) of XT

(i.e. V −1 = V T ), and Si ∈R|Ni|×n with positive values along the diagonal. In this case, our solution can be
expressed as:
βi = ViS−1
i
U T
i δif
(B.12)
This discussion justifies solving for the gradient by setting up a regression problem if the function f is
defined in Rn, but in our setup, the ordering function, l : M →R is imagined to be be defined on a d-
dimensional Riemannian manifold M embedded in Rn, with n ≫d, and thus we are including far too many
dimensions in equation (B.12) which can only be adding errors since the l(x) does not vary in these directions.
Furthermore, if n ≫1, the Euclidean metric itself begins to become less informative as the concentration
of measure phenomenon starts to take effect [56]. Given this consideration, the most principled next step
would be to try and restrict our regression problem to the tangent space TxM in which ∇l(x) actually lives;
furthermore, we know that TM is a rank-d subbundle of TRn and thus at at xi ∈D we need to restrict to a
d-dimensional affine space centred about xi. This is exactly the sort of problem that Principal Component
Analysis (PCA) is designed to solve, and thus, in what follows, we will apply PCA locally on every difference
matrix Xi for i = 1, . . . , m, to construct an approximation of the tangent bundle on M. This method is
referred to as local-PCA (for obvious reasons) and is familiar to many people working in the field of manifold
learning, as it is one of the simplest constructive methods to access the tangent spaces of a manifold [83].
Before proceeding, we remark that our restriction to the neighbourhood Ni needs to include enough points
to resolve the function locally, but not so many points that the linear approximation of βi to δil breaks down,
and also must not be so large that the curvature of the manifold invalidates using a linear approximation
to the tangent space. The determination of these neighbourhoods is either done by setting ηi ≡η to be
72


constant, as is done by Singer & Wu in [83], and which allows for some cleaner theoretical results; or by
setting ηi = ∥xi −xk
i ∥where xk
i is the kth nearest neighbour to xi not including xi, which uniformises our
neighbourhoods (i.e. |Ni| ≡k) and thus gives each estimate the same chance to fail or succeed. If the data
is very inhomogeneously sampled, then choosing the latter parameterisation is essentially necessary, and the
burden will then fall on how large you allow your neighbourhood to be; we have found that for manifolds of
relatively low dimensions k ∈[10, 30] often produces good results. We also note that it is standard to centre
Xi before applying PCA, but since we are only interested in the projection, we skip this step.
It is important to recall that we do not a priori assume we know the value of the intrinsic dimensionality
d. We could now employ one of the many intrinsic dimensionality estimation methods in the literature (see,
for example, [44, 37, 46, 32, 1, 26]), but instead we will keep within the domains of PCA and define a local
dimensionality di at every xi ∈D which will equal the minimum number of dimensions required to explain
a preset proportion of the variance in our neighbourhood Ni. This is equivalent to choosing some γ ∈(0, 1)
and defining:
di = min
(

Pk

(B.13)
where {σj}|Ni|

to now define:

so that we have a uniform estimate of the intrinsic dimensionality across the manifold; however, in practice

There are other gradient estimation methods that we could apply — most notably, a finite-difference

our assumptions about the data. Furthermore, while testing different schemes, we found that local-PCA was
the most reliable on inhomogeneously sampled data.
B.3
Heuristic Parameterisations of ODK
In this appendix, we will briefly discuss heuristic parametrisations that one might use for ODKs; we should
view these as data-adaptive parameterisations that are not related to the underlying dynamics that generated
the data but only seek to fit ODKs effectively to the data. It is worth noting that all of these heuristics will
have tangible drawbacks, and some may not work at all on certain datasets; it is important to tune — or
design new — heuristics based on the realities of the data you are studying. In what follows, D = {xi}m
i=1 ⊂
Rn will be our data, which we assume clusters around a d-dimensional submanifold, M, of Rn.
B.3.1
Geodesic Construction of an Ordering Function
We will begin by discussing the geodesic construction of the ordering function that we have mentioned several
times in the main text. First, we construct a symmetric (undirected) neighbourhood graph G = (V, E) where
a fixed indexing of our data forms the vertex set, V; and E is our edge set, i.e. i →j
⇐⇒
(i, j) ∈E,
and where i →j reads i connects to j; and we note that the property of being symmetric is equivalent to
G being undirected. It is not particularly important which graph construction method you use to construct
your graph so long as it satisfies the following three properties:
28We usually set γ = 0.9, as was done in [83], and found it suitable in almost all cases.
29Recall that the principal components are matrices are the columns of Q in the eigendecomposition XXT = QΛQ−1, where
symmetry implies Q−1 = QT ; and if XT = USV T then XXT = V S2V T , thus Q = V .
73


1). G is symmetric: ∀i, j ∈V such that (i, j) ∈E, then we must also have (j, i) ∈E;
2). G is connected: ∀i, j ∈V there exist vertices i0, i1, . . . , ik ∈V such that i0 = i and ik = j and
(il, il+1) ∈E for all l = 0, 1, . . . , k −1;
3). G is a neighbourhood graph: there exists some r > 0, that is sufficiently small with respect to the
maximum length scale of D (i.e. r ≪maxi,j∈V∥xi−xj∥), such that ∀i ∈V, we have maxj∈Ni d(xi, xj) <
r, where Ni = {j ∈V : (i, j) ∈E} are the descendants of i, and d : D × D →R is some distance metric.
As a disclaimer, we have invented the definition of a neighbourhood graph ourselves purely for convenience;
but we hope that it is clear that we are simply requiring that G encodes neighbourhood structure that is
consistent with the neighbourhood structure of the underlying manifold; and thus doesn’t allow long-distance
connections. A convenient method to represent such graphs is to use a distance threshold function f(i, j)
that defines the edge set through (i, j) ∈E ⇐⇒d(xi, xj) < f(i, j). The most common graph is a k-Nearest
Neighbours (kNN) graph where f(i, j) = ∥xi −xk
i ∥, where xk
i denotes the kth nearest neighbour to xi (not
including xi itself), but we notice that f(i, j)̸ = f(j, i) so this graph is not necessarily connected; we could
then consider the following two variations: the kNN-AND graph f(i, j) = min(∥xi −xk
i ∥, ∥xj −xk
j ∥), or the
kNN-OR graph f(i, j) = max(∥xi −xk
i ∥, ∥xj −xk
j ∥). Our personal preference is to use the Continuous-kNN
(CkNN) graph, introduced by Berry & Sauer in [13], which sets f(i, j) = δ
q
∥xi −xk
i ∥· ∥xj −xk
j ∥for some
sensitivity δ > 0; the CkNN graph has much more stable geometric properties as the dataset size increases,
and on inhomogeneously sampled data it is easier to construct a connected CkNN graph for a modest value
of k.
After one has constructed a graph, G, and given that we know a distribution, p0(x), on the initial states,
then we can construct the ordering function as:
l(xj) =
X

¯p0(xi) min
P ∈Pij
|P |−1
X
k=0
∥xik −xik+1∥
(B.16)
where ¯p0(xi) = p0(xi)/ P
j∈V p0(xj) and Pij = Sm
k=0{(i0, i1, . . . , ik) ∈Vk+1 : i0 = i ∧ik = j} is the set of all
possible paths originating at i and terminating at j. If we ¯p0(x) = ei0 for some i0 ∈V (i.e. p0(x) = δ(x−xi0)),
then the outer sum would disappear and equation (B.16) would be the standard shortest path length formula
on a graph, denoted as dG(i0, j); otherwise, we are computing the average of dG(i, j) over i. For symmetric
and connected graphs, the shortest path will always exist and be well defined, and if our discrete graph
is a neighbourhood graph, the shortest path will asymptotically approximate the geodesic distance on M.
In practice, if |supp(p0)| ≪m, you should use Dijkstra’s Algorithm [27] to compute the shortest path;
otherwise, or if at some future point you may need a matrix of pairwise geodesic distances, then you should
use the Floyd-Warshall algorithm [35, 97].
The geodesic construction of the ordering function should yield ∥∇l(x)∥≡1, so in theory there is no
need to estimate the scale of the ordering. However, in practice, inhomogeneities in in the sampling can case
the effective local scales to vary and thus one still might wish to compute κ(x) = ∥∇l(x)∥; in this case, and
if one has used a different construction of the ordering, we refer the reader to Appendix B.2 for a detailed
discussion of how we may approximate this quantity.
