B.3.3
Heuristics for ε
Now that we have made this consideration, we will begin providing heuristic methods that one can use to
determine a good parametrisation of (ε, τ(x), ρ(x)2). We will proceed by first constructing a heuristic that
30The dependence on the dimension d in this formula for m(ε, q) is a realisation of the so-called curse-of-dimensionality,
whereby to resolve our statistics we must sample exponentially more points as the dimensionality increases.
75


quantifies the degree of degeneration that occurs as the bandwidth decreases; for convenience, we will assume
that ρ(x) has already been set. We define the maximum transition probability from a sample xi as:
pi
max(ε) = max
xj∈D Pε(xi, xj)
(B.21)
where Pε is to be viewed as our row-stochastic transition matrix, not the continuous operator; we also define
pmax(ε) := maxi=1,...,m pi
max(ε). For diffusion maps kernels with monotonically decreasing choices of h(·), we
will have pi
max(ε) = Pε(xi, xi), which is the self-transition probability, or, alternatively, the probability of
not doing anything. Since Pm
j=1 Pε(xi, xj) = 1, for any fixed set of parameters we will have pmax(ε) →0 as
m →∞, so it may seem as if this metric doesn’t tell us much, but for a fixed dataset pmax(ε) →1 as ε →0.
So if we set some threshold value ηp ∈(0, 1), for example ηp = 0.9, then for a given value of ε pmax(ε) > ηp
implies there is some xi ∈D where a diffusion kernel only diffuses 10% of the time — which seems like a
poor representation of diffusion on D. Thus, we can use pmax to determine whether our bandwidth is failing
to resolve somewhere in D; setting a more reasonable threshold, such as ηp = 0.5, can allow us to define a
lower bound on our bandwidth based on a heuristic notion of what we feel determines a good fit for us; i.e.
we can define εmin = inf{ε > 0 : pmax(ε) < ηp}.31
Supposing we have restricted the transitions of our kernel (ODK or otherwise) to a symmetric, connected,
neighbourhood graph32 of the data, denoted GD = (V, ED), and noting that this should not be the same
graph you use to compute the geodesic order of equation (B.16) and should be significantly larger. We could
think of GD as only being computed for the practical purposes of reducing the computation load of the
kernels and avoiding floating-point issues when we have transition probabilities being approximately zero
(long-distance transitions). Then we can construct a heuristic metric for a maximum bandwidth (and indeed
a maximum value of τ(x)) by computing the resolution as:
resi(ε) =
X
j:(i,j)∈ED
P D
ε (xi, xj)
(B.22)
where P D
ε has not been restricted to the graph GD and is applied to the whole dataset; as before we can also
define res(ε) = maxi=1,...,m resi(ε). This computes how much detail we are losing by restricting transitions
to GD. Thus, if we are happy with the validity of our constructed graph, then we can set some threshold
ηr ∈(0, 1) and if res(ε) > ηr we know that the bandwidth is too large to be represented on our graph in
some region of the data. This allows us to define εmax = sup{ε > 0 : res(ε) > ηr}. Putting this together
with εmin = inf{ε > 0 : pmax(ε) < ηp}, we can define an interval (εmin, εmax) such that we are confident our
kernel is well defined for any value of ε in this range.
We would also like to bring attention to a nice method from Shan & Daubechies [78], that provides a
principled and easily applicable metric to select on optimal bandwidth. It is true that the family of transition
operators {Pε}ε>0 will form a semi-group under composition,33 and will satisfy the relation PsPt = Ps+t.
Our discrete realisations of Pε will not satisfy this property exactly, but choosing the value of ε where this
discrepancy — referred to as the Semi-Group Error (SGE) — is minimised gives us a transition operator
that best respects the underlying algebra of the set. Since it seems reasonable to assume that the SGE will
be minimised for values of ε which best approximate exp(εL), since these operators will trivially satisfy the
composition rule, we can assume that the minimiser of the SGE will also give us a measure of when our
approximation to the continuous Pε(x, y) is the best given our restricting to the data. For clarity, we will
state the SGE as defined in [78]:
SGE(ε) = ∥PεPε −P2ε∥
(B.23)
where in their paper they used the operator norm, but we found simpler norms, such the Frobenius norm,
worked just as well. When working with real data (as opposed to synthetic data), we have often used pmax(ε)
and res(ε) to determine a maximal range of valid ε’s, and then used SGE(ε) to localise us within this range,
and within this local region we can then perform a grid search to determine our optimal choice of ε.
31In practice, there is some flexibility in how harshly we apply this threshold, it is likely true that not all samples are equally
important to us, so we could relax this to Q0.9({pmax(i) : i = 1, . . . , m}) > ηp where Qθ is the θth quantile of a set (assuming
a uniform distribution over the values).
32Under certain circumstance it might be reasonable to relax the symmetry constraint.
33A semi-group is a set with an associative binary operation (a group without identity of inverses).
76


B.3.4
Redundancy of ODK Parametrisations
The above discussion required ρ(x) to be fixed, as otherwise the above would not relate to ε but rather
ερ(x)2, and thus we would still have to choose how to balance this weight between the two parameters. To
proceed without ambiguity, we must note that there is some redundancy in the parameter space of an ODK.
Precisely, denote by Φ = (ε, τ(x), ρ(x)) ∈R>0 × C2(M) × C2(M), and element of this parameter space,
and let KΦ be the ODK with this parameterisation; assuming l(x) and κ(x) are fixed, then we can define
the following equivalence relationship Φ1 ∼Φ2 ⇐⇒KΦ1(x, y) = KΦ2(x, y), ∀x, y ∈M, by inspecting the
definition of an isotropic ODK as given in equation (5), we see that this is equivalent to the following:
Φ1 ∼Φ2 ⇐⇒∀x ∈M, ε1τ1(x) = ε2τ2(x) ∧ε1ρ1(x)2 = ε2ρ2(x)2
(B.24)
⇐⇒∀x ∈M,
τ1(x)
ρ1(x)2 = τ2(x)
ρ2(x)2 ∧ε1τ1(x)2
ρ1(x)2
= ε2τ2(x)2
ρ2(x)2
(B.25)
Equation (B.24) shows us that discussing the heuristic parametrisation of τ(x) and ρ(x)2 without fixing ε
creates redundancy in our parameter space since the equivalence class (set of all parameters which produce
the same kernel) of any parameterisation would satisfy:

(ε, τ(x), ρ(x)2)

= {(ε′, τ ′(x), ρ′(x)2) : ∃c > 0, ε′ = ε/c, τ ′(x) = cτ(x), ρ′(x)2 = cρ(x)2}
(B.26)
If we view ε as a time-scale again, then this equation is saying that all ODKs are equivalent up to a linear
time-change. It is worth dwelling on this: in the main text we always consider ε as being a time-scale of
the underlying process that generated the data, relative to the time horizon [0, T], with T < ∞. Supposing
we have parametrised our ODK with ground truth drift and diffusion, and some ε > 0, then we must apply
the adjoint transition operator to p0 approximately S = T
ε times before our time marginals have spanned
the data entirely. Equation (B.26) tells us that if we applied a linear time change ε′ = cε, for some c > 0,
we will get an identical kernel, and thus we must still apply our reparametrised ODK S times to span our
data, which implies the new effective time horizon of the underlying process is T ′ = Sε′ = cT; and thus this
redundancy in ODKs parameter space is structurally equivalent to equating all SDEs that are equal up to a
linear time-change.
This observation provides us much more freedom in our choice of ε when we are parametrising our kernel
heuristically, since we can always transform back to a regime where ε is a real time-scale of the underlying
process.
As an example, suppose T = 5, then we might decide that given the rough scale of the data
(maximum length scale maxxi,xj∈D∥xi −xj∥, and the spread of the data Vx∼q(x)) the most likely order
of magnitude of τ(x) and ρ(x)2 would allow us to set ε∗= 0.1 as our reference time-scale. We could then
arbitrarily set ε = 1 and then heuristically estimate the values for τ(xi) and ρ(xi) — using techniques already
mentioned above and/or those that we will shortly discuss — without any consideration for the value of ε.
After we are done, we can simply transform back to the physically relevant reference time-scale regime,
and thus our kernel would be unchanged, and our new heuristic estimates would be τ ∗(xi) = τ(xi)/ε∗and
ρ∗(xi) = ρ(xi)/
√
ε∗.
It is important to note that we are not getting anything for free here; we cannot simply rescale to ε′ ≪1
and claim our convergence results are better, since only ε’s that resolve on the data and can reasonably be
interpreted as a time-scale will give the asymptotics any definite meaning with respect to the underlying
process. Furthermore, if one simply rescaled ε and T and then applied the Lq or Lp optimisation schemes,
we would get identical results as we would if we didn’t rescale, since the time horizons change identically.
The utility of this rescaling would only be realised if we intended to estimate the drift and diffusion in a
dimensionless manner based on some geometric or statistical heuristics, and where, to this end, getting rid
of the extra degree of freedom is convenient. Another scenario where this rescaling invariance is of great use,
is if we didn’t know the time-horizon of the underlying process, in this case we cannot ascribe any meaning
to ε as a timescale, making it a truly dimensionless quantity that may as well be set equal to 1.34. If one
inferred the values of τ(x) and ρ(x) in a such a manner as to match the true underlying dynamics that
generated the data (for example, you applied the Lq or Lp objective of section 4.3); then the fact that it
34We remark that this is the case because the concept of a short or long time only makes sense relative to the global time-
horizone which you are considering; i.e. a decade is a long time relative to the life of a human, but it is an exceptionally short
time relative to the age of the universe.
77


is possible to rescale ODKs to a more physical time-scale allows you to still claim you have learnt the true
underlying dynamics, just up to a multiplicative constant. Furthermore, quantities of the form τ(x)kρ(y)−2k
for any non-zero k will be invariant to linear time-changes; thus, one can talk very precisely about such
relative quantities.
We can group the equivalence conditions given in equations (B.24) & (B.25) into the following list:
Ψ(x) = τ(x)
ρ(x)2 ,
ψ(x) = ετ(x)2
ρ(x)2 ,
b(x) = ετ(x),
a(x) = ερ(x)2
(B.27)
We call Ψ(x) the drift-to-diffusion ratio, b(x) the drift-scale, and a(x) the diffusion scale; the dimensions
of each can be determined from: ετ ∼[L] - a length scale; τ ∼[L][t]−1 = [u] - rate of advection; and
ρ2 ∼[D] = [L]2[t]−1 - rate of diffusion; thus ψ(x) ∼[L][u]
[D]
= [1], is a dimensionless number in the form of
a P´eclet number [14]. A P´eclet number can be seen as a measure of the relative difference in the rate of
transport due to advection, against the rate of transport due to diffusion. We can also see that Ψ(x) ∼[L]−1
is an inverse length scale, and particularly, it is invariant under linear time changes; so fixing a functional
form for one regime will fix it for all others. This property makes Ψ(x) difficult to set heuristically, unless one
had some additional information that resulted in a restricted functional form of Ψ(x); if one were running
multiple heuristic parametrisations of ODK, then setting the functional form of Ψ(x) to be different across
each would ensure each ODK is fundamentally distinct.
It is the quantities given in equation (B.27) that we will construct heuristics for estimating. We note
that there are only two degrees of freedom in this choice, since fixing any two of these quantities determines
the others; thus, of the below-listed heuristics, we only need to apply at most two.
B.3.5
Constructing a Length Scale
If we wished to fix ετ(x) ∼[L] or ερ(x)2 ∼[L]2, we need a local estimate of the length scale in each

a good fit for the kernel by ensuring it doesn’t degenerate. The most common method to extract such a
length scale is to choose some k ∈N>0 and define ri,k = ∥xi −xk
i ∥∼[L]. It has been shown that such

our length scales more comparable over the data. Furthermore, since the length scale is estimated from
samples in a d-dimensional volume around xi, it is may be more justified to consider the geometric mean as
opposed to the arithmetic mean, since the relationship between length and volume is multiplicative; this is
indicated by the fact that the geometric mean is far less dampened estimator of the radius of data sampled
uniformly in a d-dimensional hypersphere.
Since ετ(xi) ∼[L], fixing a length scale can allow us to set one or both of ρ(xi) = cρLi and τ(xi) = cτLi,
for appropriate constants of proportionality cτ, cρ > 0. It is important to note that the length scales of
the drift and diffusion need not be the same, and setting them as such enforces a hard constraint on the
drift-to-diffusion ratio Ψ(x) ∝L−1
i , i.e. the drift-to-diffusion ratio will be adapted to the underlying density.

concentration of measure: distances will become very large and roughly symmetric about their mean. No
amount of rescaling can fix the loss of discrimination, but if you have constructed a length scale that
depends on intrinsic distributional scales, then introducing a dimension-dependent rescaling may help make
one’s kernels more interpretable.

introduce in section 3.2 to generalise ODKs to anisotropic diffusions; we will also discuss the choice of the

AB −BA; the commutator trivially satisfies that A and B commute if and only if [A, B] = 0. The Baker-
Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula (as given in [41], Chapter 3.4) gives as expansion in terms of ε for an
operator Cε such that exp(Cε) = exp(εA) exp(εB), the CBH gives this expansion as:
Cε = ε(A + B) + 1
4ε2[A, B] + 1
12ε3 ([A, [A, B]] + [B, [B, A]]) + 1
24ε4[A, [B, [B, A]]] + . . .
(B.41)
with all terms of order ε2 and higher being in terms of the commutator of A and B - a quick sanity check
then shows that if [A, B] = 0 that Cε = ε(A+B). Thus, the error of approximating Pε with P A
ε P µ
ε , is O(ε2)
in the log-space of Pε, where log Pε = log exp(εL) = εL the inverse of the operator exponential (defined in
terms of its power series). This approximation scheme is often called Lie-Trotter Splitting ([90]), and is a
generalisation of the first-order Euler method for numerical integration. Naturally, we can reduce the error
in our approximation by considering generalisations of higher-order integration methods; a generalisation of
the Verlet Scheme gives:

have no bearing on the optimal α, and that the composition scheme is not the limiting factor in making the
approximation ¯Pε,α. In hindsight this latter point is not surprising, since by Theorem 3.1, we know that the
leading order error in approximating any Pε can be as high as O(ε3/2), and thus the O(ε3) error coming from
the composition would be insignificant in comparison. This leads us to conclude that the choice of α depends
on the eigenstructure of A(x) and one should choose α as large as is possible without Pε/2,α degenerating on
the data. We also believe that the optimal order of composition for the Strang splitting scheme will depend
on which of A(x) and µ(x) is changing most rapidly on the data — and since in practice variations of µ are
likely to cause more pronounced errors we would generally recommend using P A
ε/2,αP µ
ε,αP A
ε/2,α.
B.5
Riemannian Geometry Preliminaries
In this appendix, we will give some background on the tools from differential geometry that we use throughout
this paper; we hope that this provides clarity on which differential geometric concepts are important to us
and how and why we use them.
82


We assume that we are working on a d-dimensional Riemannian manifold embedded in Rn, where n ≫d;
we usually denote this manifold as M, but when we do so, we are talking about the manifold intrinsically.
It would be more proper to say that (M, gM) is a d-dimensional Riemannian manifold with metric gM, and
there is an embedding ι : M →Rn whose image we denote N := ι(M). In differential geometry, an embed-
ding is an immersion that is also a differentiable homeomorphism onto its image (i.e. a diffeomorphism); the
latter condition means that the topological structure (the open sets) of M and N are identical, while being
an immersion (guarantees that the manifold structure is preserved through the mapping; therefor, ι being
an embedding means that N is a d-dimensional submanifold of the n-dimensional manifold Rn. In addition,
(M, gM) is (locally) a metric space, so for us to consider (M, gM) and (N, gM) is be isomorphic under ι we
need the additional condition that ι : (M, gM) →(N, gN ) be an isometry — in other words, distances on
M and N are also the same — this is equivalent to gM = ι∗gN where ι∗is the pullback and is defined as
ι∗gN (u, v) = gN (Dι(u), Dι(v)). Any calculations we make we do so on N ⊂Rn, but we assume there is an
isometric embedding ι that allows us to consider all quantities computed on N as being computed on M and
we do so without ambiguity. Furthermore, we assume that gN is inheritted from the Euclidean metric on Rn,
i.e. for any x ∈M let u, v ∈TxM, then ˜u = Dι(x)(u) and ˜v = Dι(x)(v) will be two vectors in Rn such that
ι(x)+ ˜u and ι(x)+ ˜v are tangent to N in Rn at ι(x), we assume that gN (˜u, ˜v) = ⟨˜u, ˜v⟩2 = ˜uT ˜v = u(DιT Dι)v,
and thus the metric gM = DιT Dι when written in orthonormal coordinates (projection coordinates as we
will soon call them) on N. From now on we hope that you are convinced that we may work unambiguously
on the manifold M with metric gM.
A geodesic through a point x ∈M is a curve γ : I →M, where I ⊂R is a closed interval of the
form [0, T] such that γ(0) = x and whose rate of change is constant (constant velocity), and which we
formulate as γ having no acceleration. To formalise this we need to have a notion of differentiation vector
fields which require us to be able to take differences of the form γ′(t+dt)−γ′(t), where γ′(t) is the standard
derivative of γ : I →φ(U) where (U, φ) is a coordinate chart containing γ(t) and which has a coordinate
independent meaning up to composition with the transition functions of compatible charts. The issue is
that γ′(t + dt) ∈Tγ(t+dt)M and γ′(t) ∈Tγ(t)M exist in different vector spaces and thus cannot be naively
subtracted. This results in the notion of a connection which allows us to connect different vector spaces
defined over M together; more formally, suppose that E is a vector bundle over M, and E(M) is the space
of smooth sections of E35, then a connection in E is a map ∇: T M × E(M) →E(M) which takes a section
of the tangent bundle (i.e. a vector field) and a section of the E and outputs another section of E, and must
be linear over C∞(M) in the first argument, linear in R in the second argument, and for f ∈C∞(M) must
satisfy the product rule ∇X(fY ) = f∇X(Y ) + (Xf)Y where Xf(p) = df(X(p)) is the directional derivative
of f in the direction X(p). This gives the connection the structure of a directional derivative and thus ∇XY
is called the covariant derivative of Y in the direction of X. If E(M) = T (M), i.e. E = TM is the tangent
bundle, then we call ∇: T (M) × T (M) →T (M) a linear connection on M; without going into detail,
there is a one-to-one correspondence between linear connections and choices of d3 smooth functions Γk
ij on
M, and they are related through:
∇XY = (Xi∂iY k + XiY jΓk
ij)∂k
(B.49)
The smooth functions Γk
ij are called the Christoffel symbols, and although they are written with tensor
indices, they are not a tensor. It is also true that: every smooth manifold will admit a linear connection
and this can be proved constructively; the existence of a linear connection induces (under the addition
of constraints) a unique connection on each tensor bundle T k
l M.36 The connection is the mathematical
machinery that allows us to take the derivative of a vector field along a curve: supposing ∇is a linear
connection and γ : I →M a curve, then ∇determines a unique operator Dt : T (γ) →T (γ) which maps
smooth vector fields to smooth vector fields over γ(I), that satisfies linearity over R, the product rule and
(if V ∈T (γ) is a restriction of some vector field ˜V ∈T (M)) DtV (t) = ∇γ′(t) ˜V ; we call Dt the covariant
35A rank-n vector bundle, E, over M is a set of the form E = F
x∈M Dx where each Dx has the structure of a vector space
and all Dx have dimension n; a smooth section of a bundle is a smooth function V : M →E : x 7→(x, v) where v ∈Dx, i.e. a
section of a bundle maps points of M to vectors in E.
36This notation defines the bundle of all type
 k
l

-tensors across M.
A
 k
l

-tensor can be viewed as a multilinear map
acting on k-covariant vectors and l-contravariant vectors; we recall that co- and contravariant vectors are determined by the
transformation properties of the vector with respect to a change in coordinates, where covariant vectors transform identically
to the coordinate basis and contravariant vectors inversely to the coordinates.
83


derivative along γ. A vector field V is said to be parallel along γ if DtV ≡0, if V is parallel along any curve
then we just say V is parallel, it is true that for any curve γ and V0 ∈Tγ(0)M that there is a unique parallel
vector field V along γ such that V (γ(0)) = V0; the uniqueness in our statement allows us to unambiguously
transport a vector V0 along γ to a vector V (γ(T)) ∈Tγ(T )M.
We already have enough machinery to describe a geodesic, but we will deal with two further technicalities
first. The first issue is that if X, Y ∈TpM and we take a short path parallel to X, taking us to x, and
then from x take another short path parallel to Y to get to q, supposing the short paths we the same, would
r ∈M — the point reached after taking the short path parallel to Y to get to y, and then the short path
parallel X at y — be the same as q? It turns out that this need not be true, and the extent of this failure can
be quantified by the torsion tensor τ(X, Y ) = ∇XY −∇Y X −[X, Y ], where [·, ·] is the Lie-Bracket of vector
fields (or equivalently the commutator when viewing a vector field as an operator on smooth functions).
For any connection on a Euclidean space τ(X, Y ) ≡0, so we will assume that our chosen connection ∇
satisfies this as well — in such a case the linear connection is called symmetric, and this is equivalent to
the associated Christoffel symbols satisfying Γk
ij = Γk
ji. The final constraint we need is that our chosen
connection is compatible with the metric, i.e. ∇Xg(Y, Z) = g(∇XY, Z) + g(Y, ∇XZ), this is equivalent to:
∇g ≡0; if V, W are parallel vector fields along a curve γ, then g(V, W) is constant; parallel translation
is an isometry. Thus, we can invoke the fundamental lemma of Riemannian geometry, which states: for a
Riemannian manifold (M, g), there is a unique linear connection ∇that is compatible with g and symmetric
— we call this unique connection the Levi-Civita connection.
As a result, we can define a geodesic through a point x ∈M as a curve γ : I →M originating at x and
satisfying Dt ˙γ ≡0 where Dt is with respect to the unique symmetric connection compatible with gM. Given
x ∈M and v ∈TxM there exists a unique maximal geodesic γv : I →M such that γ(0) = x and ˙γ(0) = v.
We can define a subset of the tangent bundle E := {V ∈TM : γV is defined on the interval [0, 1]} ⊂TM in
which the so-called exponential map can be naturally defined exp : E →M : V 7→γV (1), with expx being
the exponential map restricted to TxM (i.e. E ∩TxM). Two important properties of the exponential map
are: it commutes over isometries, i.e. ι ◦expx = expι(x) ◦Dι; there exists a neighbourhood V ⊂TxM of the
origin, and a neighbourhood U of x in M such that expx : V →U is a diffeomorphism, any neighbourhood U
in which this property holds is called a normal neighbourhood. If {ei}d
i=1 form an orthonormal basis of TxM
with respect to the metric g = gM, then we can define the isomorphism E : Rd →TxM : (x1, . . . , xd) 7→
xiei, if U is a normal neighbourhood of x then φ := E−1 ◦exp−1
x
: U →Rd defines a coordinate chart,
the respective coordinates are called the normal coordinates at x. Expressing things in terms of normal
coordinates flattens U in the most canonical manner; thus, many Euclidean intuitions hold true in these
coordinates. In particular, if we write the metric in these coordinates, we will find gij = δij, i.e. the metric
appears flat.
We can define another set of coordinates on M that flattens the metric using the fact that N = ι(M) ⊂
Rn, thus we can view TxM, for any x ∈M, as being an affine subspace of Rn, i.e. a d-dimensional subspace
Wx ≤Rn and TxM ∼= ι(x) + Wx, and let us denote TxN = x + Wx. Taking any orthonormal basis {¯ei}d
i=1
of Wx, noting that ¯ei ∈Rn and with orthonormality being in the sense of the Euclidean inner product, then
defining the projection operator Px : Rn →Rd : v1¯e1 + . . . vd¯ed 7→(v1, . . . , vd) we can define the coordinate
chart ϕx : U →Rd : y 7→Px(ι(y) −ι(x)), where U is an open neighbourhood of x such that Px is injective on
ι(U); the existence of such a neighbourhood is guaranteed by the implicit function theorem. We call these
coordinates the projection coordinates, and note that unlike the normal coordinates, they depend on the
embedding ι.
If we define the normal coordinates, (s1, . . . , sd), and projection coordinates, (u1, . . . , ud), with respect
to the same orthonormal basis — i.e. Dι(e1) = ¯e1 using the previous notation — then these two coordinate
charts are naturally related. The projection coordinates take the best possible affine approximation to N
at some ι(x), whereas the normal coordinates, by nature of their implicit definition on M, will curve along
N, we could think of the normal coordinates as being the shadow of the projection coordinates projected
down onto N; hence they are highly related but not identical equal to one another. We now make a very
practical note that is relevant to us: when we talk about derivatives of functions or write the norm ∥x −y∥2
we are imagining to be doing this directly on the manifold M with respect to normal coordinates; but it is
difficult to perform some of these computations exactly (such as the expansion of Gεf(x) given in section 3)
in these coordinates since the exponential map is non-linear, but since the projection coordinates are truly
flat on N, it is much more preferable to change from normal coordinates to projection coordinates.
84


To change to coordinates in the integral of Gεf(x) given in the proof of Theorem 3.1 in Appendix A, we
must expand ∥y −x∥2 and dy in terms of projection coordinates, and we must convert the Taylor expansion
of f(y) and l(y) in normal coordinates, to being in terms of projection coordinates. Doing the former allows
us to compute the integrals we need, and the latter combines intrinsic derivatives (calculated using normal
coordinates) with projection coordinates, allowing us to derive our Laplacian L. In the remainder of this
subsection, we will overview some other relevant concepts from differential geometry.
In section 3.2, we discuss a non-isotropic extension of ODK and consider the metric on M to be non-
trivial; and in section 4.2, we consider a non-isometric embedding, which also results in having to consider a
non-trivial metric. There will still exist normal coordinates on M such that gij = δij, but these will not align,
as before, with the projection coordinates we implicitly use; therefore, the metric must remain non-trivial.
This adds complexity to the derivatives, where now the differential of a scalar function df ∈T ∗M, and the
gradient grad(f) =: ∇gf must be compatible with the metric through the following relation
df(v) = g(∇gf, v) ⇐⇒∂ifvi = gij∇i
gfvj ⇐⇒∇i
gf = gij∂jf
(B.50)
where ∂if are the standard partial derivatives of f with respect to some coordinates, and gij is the inverse
metric tensor satisfying gikgkj = δi
j. This introduces the idea of using the metric to raise or lower and index;
this is often denoted using musical notation with “X-flat” being the lowering of the index of contravariant
vector X, with X♭= gijXjdxi, and “ω-sharp” being the raising of the index of a covariant vector ω,
with ω♯= gijωj∂i; thus, we can see that ∇gf = df ♯. The divergence will also change as is again defined
implicitly through d(iXdV ) = (divgX)dV , where dV =
p
| det g|dx1 ∧· · · ∧dxd is the volume form and
iXω(V1, . . . , Vk−1) = ω(X, V1, . . . , Vk−1) is in the interior multiplication operator taking k-forms to (k −1)-
forms, so if X is your vector field then iXdV will take the parallelepiped defined by the volume for dV
break it into a sum of each side of it not including dxi scaled by Xi, so is the sum of scaled area elements;
d(iXdV ) asks how all these area elements are changing and glues it back together into another volume
form; the divergence is a measure of how much rescaling by X managed to change the volume form and
can be found to be equal to divg(X) = |g|−1/2∂i(|g|1/2Xi). The Laplace-Beltrami operator is defined to be
∆gf = divg(∇gf) and thus we can write this in coordinates as:
∆gf =
1
√

p
|g|gij∂jf

(B.51)
When our metric is trivial, we will have gij = δij and |g| ≡1 and thus ∆gf = ∂i∂jf, so we arrive back at
the regular Euclidean Laplacian, but if we cannot trivialise our metric, then we must account for how the
metric affects our derivatives. In general, the non-trivial backwards Laplacian will equal:
Lgf = µ(f) + 1

Since we still implicitly use the projection coordinates, the Laplacians that we approximate will no longer
respect the more complex Laplacians above, as they will not incorporate the change of the metric of the
manifold. Local Kernels introduced a method to approximate, under mild constraints, ∆g by constructing a
symmetrised kernel ¯Kε(x, y) = Kε(x, y)+Kε(y, x); and we, in section 3.2, construct the full Laplacians given
above under harder constraints, namely, we ask that µeff := (∂igij + 1
2gij∂i log |g|)∂j, which is the effective
advection term introduced by ∆g be such that ∃a, φ ∈C∞(M) satisfying µeff = a∇φ which allows us to use
extensions of ODK such as mODK, stODK, or the density α-regularised ODK, which introduce terms in this
form and which can then combine with the non-isotropic ODK to form the above two Laplacian operators.
The last piece of machinery from differential geometry that we discuss is the second fundamental form,
which allows us to quantify the curvature of a manifold within the context of an embedding into another,
larger manifold, which for us would be ι : M →Rn. Suppose ¯∇is the standard covariant derivative on
Rn, then the second fundamental form defined as II : T (M) × T (M) →N(M) : (X, Y ) 7→( ¯∇XY )⊥,
i.e. we take the normal part of the covariant derivative of Y in the direction of X in Rn and is naturally
85


then a measure of curvature.
The trace of the second fundamental form is called the mean curvature
vector and is denoted as H(x) = Pd
i=1 II(x)(∂i, ∂i). This is relavant to us because the mean displacement
ˇvmd(xi) = Pm
j=1 Pε(xi, xj)(xj −xi) = Lει(xi), where Lι = (Lι1, . . . , Lιn)T , and thus ˇvmd = Lει ≈v+ 1
2ρ2∆ι,
where v is the underlying velocity and 1
2ρ2 a diffusion and ∆ι(x) = H(x) the mean curvature vector of the
embedding (this is proven on Pg. 41 of [18]). By the definition of the second fundamental form giving values
in the normal bundle of ι(M), the trace — which is then a sum of normal vectors — will also point normal
to ι(M) in Rn and thus the mean displacement has a normal component that encodes the curvature of the
manifold; in section 4.2 we introduce two estimators that remove this normal component, allowing you to
reconstruct the underlying dynamics.

where τ(x) is simply a weighted sum of all K eigenfunctions, l(x) is a weighted sum of all but the trivial
(constant) eigenfunction (thus building into the parametrisation our knowledge that ODKs are invariant
to addition by a constant), and ρ(x) is the exponential of the weighted sum of all K eigenfunctions. The
purpose of log-transforming the ρ’s is to ensure the noise is bounded away from zero; other functions could
also be used to enforce positivity, such as x2 + ρmin, but while testing we found the ρ = exp(·) to be the
most reliable. We could also consider taking the modulus, ReLU, or exponential of our decomposition for
τ(x) since if we have fixed the ordering we might want to stop the process from going backwards.
Other than acting as an almost parameter-free smoothness regularisation, taking a Laplacian eigenbasis
also massively reduces the number of parameters that we have to optimise for, thus improving the stability
and rate of convergence of both our Lq and Lp optimisation problems. In figure 7, Panels B and E show
the results of applying this parameterisation framework to data sampled from the NL process of section
4.3, we can see that the estimate is still relatively noisy indicating our choice of K = 250 was too high,
but we also see that upon smoothing we have very nearly perfectly captured the true noise function. This
is noteworthy because the gradient- and Laplacian-regularised optimisation didn’t capture this structure as
faithfully, showing that the signal was weak enough that even a small amount of additional regularisation was
enough to distort the minima; thus, using a Laplacian eigendecomposition might allow us to more faithfully
reconstruct the weak signal from the diffusion in the data.
We note that, as discussed in section 3.2.1, we could replace the symmetric diffusion maps kernel with a
symmetric variable bandwidth kernel:

B.7
Identifiability of Drift and Diffusion Coefficients
Recovering the drift and diffusion from data sampled from an SDE is a classical inverse problem and is central
to many applied domains. However, it is not obvious whether the problems we pose for our optimisers to
solve are well-posed; the theory of identifiability for these problems seeks to answer the question of whether,
given some idealised form of our data, it would be theoretically possible to infer the exact parameters of
our underlying process; more precisely, let P = {Pθ : θ ∈Θ} be the set of all possible models we could
90


learn given the parameter space Θ, then — under the assumption that our model is expressive enough to
represent the observed realities — if the mapping from θ 7→Pθ is injective (i.e. Pθ1 = Pθ2
=⇒θ1 = θ2
for all θ1, θ2 ∈Θ) then our model is said to be identifiable (see [57], Chapter 1, Definition 5.2). We are to
imagine this as follows: under the assumption that, given some data D, our model can learn the optimal
parameterisation, then if our problem is identifiable, our learned model will be a faithful representation of
the underlying system.
For the inference of parameters for SDEs, the difficulty of this identifiability problem depends on the data
modalities available. For example, if sample paths (samples of whole trajectories) are observed, then the
quadratic variation along these trajectories will determine the diffusion coefficients, and the Kramers-Moyal
expansion will determine the drift. However, if we are not able to sample trajectories, and can instead only
sample independent snapshots of the process — which is the canonical setting of scRNA-seq data, where
in order to measure the gene expression of a cell you must destroy it — then the problem of parameter
identifiability is tremendously important as fundamentally our problem may be ill-posed and we would not
wish to expend energy on a futile problem. It is datasets of this sort that we wished to study in this paper
— principally, we aimed to design a kernel which was theoretically well-posed to represent the underlying
dynamics of data generated by a stochastic process, but could be easily tuned and reparametrised by users.
From the perspective of identifiability, we have a fundamentally under-determined problem; writting the
Fokker-Planck equation in divergence form ∂tpt = −∇· Jt, where Jt = µpt −1
2∇(σ2pt) is the probability
current, we can see that observations of the marginals will only determine Jt up to the addition of a
divergence-free field, and hence drift and diffusion may be exchanged without altering {pt}t∈[0,T ]. To make
progress with such problems, we need to make some structural restrictions; for example, restricting the drift
to be generated by a potential b = −∇U allows us to avoid the divergence-free gauge freedom since non-linear
gradients cannot generate divergence-free terms.
The theory of identifiability for gradient-flow SDEs with constant isotropic diffusions is now relatively
complete, with parameters being identifiable, under various sets of constraints, from the observation of as
few as three time marginals. If one has a known, or highly constrained, diffusion, then it has been (see, for
example, [42]) that the potential is recoverable from time marginals; Lavenant et al. [55] established that
the full law on paths is identified through a minimum-entropy variational principle, with a single sample per
time point sufficing when the measurement times are dense in [0, T]; Chizat et al. [19] designed a convergent
mean-field Langevin algorithm which was later extended by Neklyudov et al. [63] to time-inhomogeneous
known diffusivity. More relevant to our own work is the result of Guan et al. [39] who showed that a gradient-
flow SDE with unknown constant isotropic diffusion is jointly identifiable if and only if your observations
occur out of steady state, and furthermore, one only needs to observe three time marginals to guarantee
uniqueness.
Our Lq objective, given in equation (100), would likely only be identifiable in this restricted domain of
gradient flows and constant isotropic diffusions, but we note that our assumptions are significantly weaker
than knowing all time marginals. We assume that we know the initial distribution p0(x), the time-sampling
distribution r(t), and the transient sampling distribution q(x) =
R T
0 r(t)
R
X0 pt(x|x0)p0(x0)dx0dt. To show
identifiability, we must show that given two processes have the same initial condition, they can only produce
the same transient distribution if they have identical time-marginals for all t ∈[0, T], which then by the
result of Guan et al., we know that the processes must be identical. This might not be as infeasible as it first
seems, since the gradient dynamics precludes recurrence and thus if our model is to add probability to the
estimated transient distribution at a given sample x, it only really has one chance to do so — i.e. it must add
all the required probability exactly when the probability is flowing through this point. Furthermore, every
path evolved up to some t ∈[0, T] will have quadratic variation equal to tσ2, so if this diffusion isn’t properly
tuned, the model’s estimated variability will be incongruous with the observed data. Thus, we conjecture
that the spread of our observed data determines the magnitude of the diffusion, and the geometry determines
the drift; by constraining ourselves to gradient-flow SDEs with constant isotropic diffusion we believe, as
with existing results, these determining factors separate cleanly, giving us some form of identifiability.
However, if we wish to relax the condition of constant isotropic noise, the problem becomes significantly
more challenging, as the signals from the drift and diffusion can begin to mix together and one’s model must
not only be able to identify the correct regime, but then at the finer scale, must untangle the balance between
the drift and diffusion. As we have mentioned several times in the main text, when the diffusion is state-
dependent, you effectively introduce an effective advection term originating from the diffusion; considering
91


the Fokker-Planck equation ∂tpt = L∗pt, we have:
∂tpt = −∇·

(B.93)
Hence, the model must be able to isolate by which mechanism the probability is pushed forward. A real
experimental demonstration of this was given by Hoffmann et al. [48] who showed that one could model
the gene expression of a differentiating stem cell — a process usually modeled with ODEs — using an
entirely diffusive model; thus, if your system is likely generated by a process with state-dependent noise,
then understanding when you can, or cannot, reasonably identify ones parameters is critical. We can also see
how state-dependent noise produces issues with identifiability by assuming that L∗

when diffusion is constant Guan et al. exploit the fact that the diffusions factor outside of the Laplacian
as

results for known diffusions would guarantee the potentials be equal [55]; now since the residual diffusion
is non-degenerate and the residual drift −∇(Ψ1 −Ψ2) is Lipschitz and satisfies linear growth, thus we can
instantiate a uniqueness result from PDE theory (namely, Theorem 4.1.6, of [15]) to prove that pt = p0
for all t ∈[0, T] which contradicts the system not being in steady state. However, if we cannot make this
factorisation, we have one PDE, namely 0 = (L∗

2)pt, in two unknowns, giving us an underdetermined
PDE which precludes the application of existing uniqueness results for PDEs. It was shown by Cristofol
et al. [23] that a general univariate SDE is identifiable if one observes the time evolution of the first- and
second-order moments over some time interval; this result is intuitive given our previous discussion since we
are now trying to derive uniqueness from two PDEs in terms of the forward operators, (L1 −L2)xt = 0 and
(L1 −L2)x2
t = 0.
Our Lp objective is posed towards this significantly more challenging problem where one has state-
dependent noise; furthermore, we still assume that we have relatively little dynamical information, namely,
it is assumed that we know the initial time-marginal p0(x), and we observe independent samples of trajectories
and when, along these trajectories, they were sampled. As a result, we have effectively observed the transient
distribution q(x), the time sampling distribution, r(t), and a very noisy representation of the joint distribution
p(x, t). The results we obtained from applying the Lp objective to data sampled from the highly complex NL
process showed empirically that we can faithfully reconstruct the first-order deterministic dynamics while
simultaneously approximating the diffusion. However, our estimates of the diffusion were highly noisy, and
the functional changes observed by introducing a very small smoothness regularisation indicate that the
signal from the diffusion, given this objective, is very weak, but nonetheless it is present. In Panel F of
figure 7, we show the ground truth and estimated noise functions plotted against the ground truth ordering
function (which gives us a measure of how far along the process we are), we observe that our Laplacian
Eigenbasis estimate, although noisy, very faithfully captures the noise (after some ad hoc smoothing), even
features that would seem unimportant given the form of our objective, i.e. the small but sharp increase in
diffusion outside of ∥x∥> 2π. However, this estimate was highly noisy and required ad hoc smoothing to
appreciate that it really had captured the signal from the diffusion; this indicates that the loss landscape
is very flat around the minima, which we interpret as evidence of some weak identifiability. The objective
function is isolating the noise by using ODKs to numerically integrate the Fokker-Planck equation; given
that we observe sampling times, if the path integral of the diffusion has been too small, then the observed
uncertainty in the sampling times would be misaligned with the numerical integration. Thus, we conjecture
that the Lp objective, computed by numerically integrating ODKs, is capable of untangling deterministic and
stochastic contributions to the dynamics by adaptively matching the mean-field dynamics and the uncertainty
in sampling times. It is not clear if this is sufficient in general, but it may provide some evidence that,
potentially with additional constraints, that gradient-flow SDEs with state-dependent isotropic diffusions
are identifiable from observations of the joint distribution p(x, t) and the initial distribution p0(x), under
the assumption that the process is not in steady-state — it may be required that we assume that the time-
sampling distribution has a specific form, such as uniform or exponentially distribution; it also may be
necessary to assume that one knows the steady-state distribution and this is reached at time T, i.e. we also
may need to know pT (x). The assumption that one is not in steady-state is essential, since otherwise the
SDE dxt = −∇Ψ(xt)dt + σdwt, and the rescaled SDE dyt = −α∇Ψ(xt)dt + √ασdwt, would have identical
time-marginals, and thus one has a counterexample for identifiability.
